Risk, Compliance and Audit Update

Risk

3.1

Risk Dashboard: Mark Baldock introduced the paper, which had been circulated
previously and was taken as read. In response to questions from the Committee, it
was explained that the enterprise risk structure mirrors the Orange Book of Risk
Management and was thematic. It was not expected that the risks would change from
one month to another. It was the linked risks where the detail changed more
frequently: there have been 72 new linked risks over the last three months and 100
linked risks were closed at an intermediate and local level. The question was when
the changes in these linked risks impacted the enterprise risk and there needed to be

a material change to impact the enterprise risk rating. The Chair highlighted that the
dashboard needed to better highlight any changes in risk vs. the ongoing
management of an existing risk. Ben Foat further commented that risk scores were
given but the numbers were not defined. It was also highlighted that Declan Salter
should be identified against the GLO risk and the impact score for the Starling and
GLO risk should be five, not three. It was agreed that Mark Baldock would make these
changes to the risk dashboard, including putting any new risks to the front of the
dashboard moving forwards (from January 2021) and provide a verbal update on
these elements to the Audit, Risk & Compliance Committee (ARC) in November.

The Committee otherwise NOTED the Risk Dashboard for onward submission to the
Audit, Risk & Compliance Committee (ARC).

3.2

Risk Policy: The Committee approved the Risk Policy, incorporating the approach to
Risk Appetite, for onward submission to the ARC, subject to the following changes:
- Compliance was to be added to the second line of defence.
- It was to be made clear that the policy applied to the Historical Matters Unit
(HMU).
- The Board and ARC were not first line defence entities but sat above the
defence line structure so should be removed as first line defence entities.
- The Committee was the be added to the Risk Governance Structure,
- The operational detail included on page 13 would be taken out placed in the
Risk Management Framework such that it could be changed if needed without
needing to revise the policy.

It was also confirmed that the policy did not apply to Payzone Bill Payments Limited
and Post Office Insurance, although these entities will have regard to the policy. Both
were to adopt their own Risk Policies and despite Post Office Insurance having its own
ARC, the Group ARC still oversaw both entities with reports from Post Office Insurance
to the Group ARC. Material risks for Post Office Insurance were also included in the
Risk Dashboard.

Action:

Compliance

3.3

Jonathan Hill introduced the paper, which had been circulated previously and was
taken as read. The specific items below were highlighted and discussed by the
Committee.

Telecoms Comms Incident: Jonathan Hill confirmed that this was the fourth or fifth
regulatory breach seen in the Telecoms area. Whilst it was serious, the number of
customers impacted was low and it was hoped the penalty would be minimised. It
was not clear how much the fine could be as it could be up to 10% of revenue but it
was expected to be less than the fine for the Text Rely issue which was £175,000 as
the present incident was not as serious, took less time to resolve and affected less
customers. Meredith Sharples explained that the breach was caused by a Fujitsu
system failure attributed to file size issues. The breach protocol was immediately
instigated and the team engaged with Ofcom. The number of customers impacted
was small and the team was working to recompense. Fujitsu were to bear the cost
for the breach and a proposal was expected from them later this week. The Chair
congratulated Meredith Sharples on dealing with the issue quickly but highlighted that
the current contractual situation was untenable (the contract and systems were seven
years old and there was no appropriate compliance team). Ultimately, what happens
next depended on the on-going strategic discussions regarding the possible sale of
the Telecoms business.

Branch registration errors: In response to questions from the Committee, it was
confirmed that it was hoped there may little or no penalty for branches completing
Travel Money transactions after having been de-registered from HMRC.

Money Service Business/Capita: It has been identified that high risk suspicious cash
deposits from a Money Service Business (a money transmitter) were being accepted,
via the bill payment reseller client Capita. The Chair highlighted that a decision had
been taken to not use Money Service Businesses and therefore this practice must
stop as soon as possible. Jonathan Hill explained that he was meeting with Capita
next week (w/c 16 November 2020) and that they would be told to stop and a timeline
would be agreed. Jonathan Hill indicated that the business would be seeking
immediate termination and the Chair agreed with this approach.

Compliance Monitoring: The Chair questioned whether it was right to pause mystery
shopping due to the recent lockdown measures. Jonathan Hill explained that the
decision was taken partly for safety (so mystery shoppers did not have to travel and
go into branches), but also because no one was buying travel insurance due to the
lockdown. As such, mystery shopping would recommence when travel was permitted.

Anti-bribery and Corruption Training: It was explained that completion by the HR
business unit was at 81.7% (compared to 96.5% across the business) (subject to
revised figures being provided). Lisa Cherry was asked to ensure colleagues in her
area completed this training as soon as possible and Jonathan Hill would provide her
with the latest list of those who had not yet completed it.

Whistleblowing Update: There were no thematic concerns and it was explained that
the majority have been received from agent assistants and relate to allegations
concerning Postmaster activity. A more centralised approach to Postmaster reported
concerns was being taken (separate from the whistleblowing process) so as to
ascertain any broad themes.

Access to Cash: The Committee asked that the draft of the responses to the call for
evidence on Access to Cash were circulated to the Group Executive (GE).

PCI-DSS and Cyber Security Update

PCI-DSS Programme Update

Jeff Smyth introduced the paper, which had been circulated previously and was taken
as read. IN response to questions from the Committee, it was confirmed the Ingencio

jitsu worked on a time/materials basis and the programme has previously signalled
a £1.2m cost risk to the ARC. The team were now requesting some changes to the
solution so more funding would be required and this was presently being costed.
However, the programme was making good progress and was hitting its milestones.
It was on track to go through accreditation and the payment solution had already
passed accreditation with Global Payments. The Committee otherwise NOTED the
PCI-DSS Programme Update for onward submission to the ARC.

Cyber Security

4.2

Tony Jowett introduced the paper, which had been circulated previously and was
taken as read. The Committee raised concerns about the number of people who had
skipped the phishing training (178) and had been lured by the fake phishing campaign
(391). It was explained that the numbers tended to go up and down a little, but
ultimately, there was no real progress and as such the team were doing a deeper root
cause analysis. The “volume” would also be “turned up” on the phishing comms. It
was also highlighted that phishing has continued to be more prevalent in the external
environment, particularly with COVID-19 scams.

It was requested that Tony Jowett provide a list of all individuals who have not done
the training to the relevant GE members. Jeff Smyth and Lisa Cherry also agreed to
flag the need for comms with Nick Read and highlight the issue with the Leadership
Group. Tony Jowett was further asked to update the paper for the ARC reflecting the
action that was being taken.

Tony Jowett also noted that his team were looking at tool to monitor culture shift and
it was agreed that he would liaise with HR on the on-going culture work to build this
into the business’ longer term defence plans. The Committee otherwise NOTED the
Cyber Security Update for onward submission to the ARC.

Belfast Data Center (Horizon) Disaster Recovery Post Test Briefing

4.4

Gary Walker introduced the paper, which had been circulated previously and was
taken as read. It was explained that each year the business is required to test disaster
recovery at the Data Center in Belfast and this took place in August 2020. The test
was partially successful with most transactions (48/52) being able to be traded out
of the secondary site. Three transaction types failed and had to use the primary site.
Post testing, the team have identified all root causes and implemented fixes. Smaller
tests have then been carried out to check the fixes have worked. The current plan
was to not to do another test of this size and scale this financial year but to ensure
that as part of Belfast Exit there was equivalent activity in suitable point i.e. latter
point of the next calendar year. The Chair commented that this was one of the
business’ biggest risks and felt that another test should be done at Easter given the
recent test was only partially successful. Jeff Smyth explained that the test was
expensive (circa £75,000) and also destroys all change programme activity hosted in
the secondary data center. The Chair noted that £75k was a tiny sum compared to
the cost and reputational damage of losing branch trading. It was agreed that the GE
should agree the timing of the next test. The Committee otherwise NOTED the paper
for onward submission to the ARC.

Suspense Accounts

Mark Underwood introduced the paper, which had been circulated previously and was
taken as read. In response to questions from the Committee, it was outlined that
since submission of the paper advice had been received from HSF and Peters & Peters.
This confirmed that the outcome of the review of suspense accounts did not mean
that the Historical Shortfalls Scheme needed to be altered, but that investigators
could consider issues relating to Moneygram and cheques. The Chair questioned
whether the KPMG report could and should be published. Mark Underwood explained
that a decision could be taken to disclose the entire report or a redacted version.
There was a question around the extent to which the report and its findings could
trigger disclosure obligations, although the vast majority of the report was not
disclosable. Mark Underwood was requested to ask the GE whether they wished the
report to be fully disclosed or a redacted version. (Post meeting note: Mark
Underwood has advised that the ARC would be asked to discuss whether the KMPG
report should be disclosed at its November 2020 meeting).

In conclusion, Mark Underwood explained that KPMG’s view was that any further
investigation would not uncover anything more and as such no further work on this
topic was recommended. The Committee otherwise NOTED the report for onward
submission to the ARC.

Historical Matters Unit (HMU) Governance Review

Graham Hemmingway and Declan Salter introduced the paper, which had been
circulated previously and was taken as read. It was explained that much progress
had been made to set up the Governance of the new HMU, but it was not yet finalised.
It was noted that this should be made clear in the paper to the ARC. Further Ben Foat
explained that the HMU would follow the usual business policies and would only have
an exemption in terms of litigation. Notably Post Office has not signed up to the Crown
Services legal panel so this should be corrected. It was further noted that work was
ongoing to address the backfill and funding as business-as-usual resource was
continuing to support the HMU. The Committee otherwise NOTED the paper for
onwards submission to the ARC.

Action:
GH

10.

Post Office Insurance Financial Conduct Authority Dual Pricing Change
Impact

Ed Dutton introduced the report, which had been circulated previously and was taken
as read. It was explained that the recent prohibition on “Price Walking” (competitive
new business prices followed by renewal prices increasing during contract) in Motor
and Home Insurance blows the traditional insurance business model out the water
and fundamentally means you cannot charge new customers different prices to
existing customers. It was expected that this prohibition would come into other
markets in due course. Post Office Insurance was still working through what this
meant for the business, but it was thought that there would still be opportunities for
competition as there would still be different views as to what a customer would cost.
A new business policy would also have a positive value, unlike before. There were
also strict rules on avoidance, but you can give vouchers for money off. The Chair
requested that the piece be brought back to the GE in due course from a commercial
and strategic perspective. At present, Post Office Insurance was still working through
what the changes meant for them and a Board Committee was being set up. The
impact would be clearer by January 2021. The Committee otherwise NOTED the
paper for onward submission to the ARC.

11.

Committee Terms of Reference

as read. The Committee APPROVED the draft Terms of Reference for onward
submission to the GE, subject to the following amendments:
- It should be specifically called out that the Committee has oversight of the
HMU; and
- The membership should be updated to include: Alisdair Cameron, Ben Foat,
Jeff Smyth, Lisa Cherry, Amanda Jones and Cathy Mayor.

13.

Deepdive: Transformation Office Change Update 2020

Dan Zinner introduced the paper, which had been circulated previously and was taken
as read. It was explained that the ARC had asked for programme update and
specifically wanted to know more about the Change Controls Framework. Saira
Burwood further outlined that the paper was seeking ARC support for the future work
to ensure sponsors and project leads were aware of their obligations under the change
lifecycle. Ultimately, the paper was an update on what had been achieved thus far
and the focusses for the next few months. The Committee NOTED the paper and the
only comment was that it might benefit from being shortened.

14.

Payzone Risk Report

Andrew Goddard introduced the paper, which had been circulated previously and was
taken as read. On the whole, the risk management in Payzone had improved and
there was now a better culture alongside monthly reviews of risks. The only area of
concern was network resilience (cyber security) and it was confirmed that Post Office
It has not yet done a comprehensive review of the Payzone network, but Andrew
Goddard explained there had been disaster recovery work done for its Vodafone
datacentres. A “hack” into the system was scheduled but had been postponed due to
COVID-19. Ben Foat noted the good progress Payzone had made with respect of
adopting Group Policies and questioned what operational processes were in place to
implement the policies. Andre Goddard confirmed that policy implementation was part
of management responsibilities and policies were being built into ways of working,
but the work was on-going. Mark Baldock further noted that work with the Payzone
Risk team had also gone well and the team were using the Post Office GRC tool. The
Committee otherwise NOTED the Payzone risk report for onward submission to the
ARC.

15.

Law & Trends Update

Ben Foat introduced the paper, which had been circulated previously and was taken
as read. The main issue that was highlighted related to the HMRC announcement in
September 2020 that VAT should be charged on settlement payments made to third
parties. The impact of this on recent settlements made to Postmasters as part of GLO
and otherwise was still being worked through and was further complicated by the
fact that some Postmasters had been employees. There was a need to work through
all settlement payments made since 2016. The Department of Business, Energy and
Industrial Strategy (BEIS) and UKGI were already aware. The Committee otherwise
NOTED the Law & Trends Update for onward submission to the ARC.

Jonathan Hill introduced the paper, which had been circulated previously and was
taken as read. It was explained that the update was requested by the ARC after the
31,000 boxes came to light that were previously unknown to the wider business to
consider the broader lens of data management and accountabilities. At present, the
remediation plan was outlined, but the sampling of the boxes had not yet started.
The challenge was the way Post Office currently structures its data and information

The Committee felt that the business needed to be sure that there was no data that
was otherwise unknown to the wider business being stored elsewhere. The following
points were discussed:

- The Committee requested that Procurement check that there are no other
contracts for data storage or archiving other than those with the Postal
Museum and Oasis.

- Writing out to branches for data might not be successful but that Tracy
Marshall (Postmaster Effectiveness Director) was to take ownership of these
requests to branches and Jonathan Hill was already working with Tracy to
agree how best to do this.

- A Record Retention programme had been completed earlier this year and
person was being undertaken to realign data owners to new roles.

- An individual needed to be assigned to go into each admin building to check
cupboards and rooms to ensure there was no data that was unknown.

- Jeff Smyth highlighted that this work could never be complete unless
electronic data was also considered (which would include digitising paper
records and tagging appropriately).

- Rod Williams explained that colleague’s emails when they leave can be
retrieved, but OneDrive data was harder to retrieve as this was deleted very
quickly. There needed to be debate on what was kept and for how long
considering data protection obligations.

Accordingly, the Legal, Data Protection, IT and Ruk Shah (MI & Analytics Director)
were asked to work on a coherent recommendation on how the data retention policy
should evolve and how it will be implemented. This should return to the RCC and ARC
in January 2021. Further, the current paper needed to articulate the next steps and
process that will be worked through.

The Committee otherwise NOTED the report and APPROVED the next steps of:
to complete the remediation activity on an expedited basis in coordination with
the Post-Conviction Disclosure Exercise and Historical Shortfall Scheme
Activity;
- for the legal team to conclude the agreed actions in respect of the assessment
of all boxes stored at Oasis, on behalf of Post Office; and
- for the GE to agree overall data governance accountability;
for onward submission to the ARC.

It was then explained that the ARC approved the following approach to Procurement BB
Risk Exceptions in September 2020:
1. Subthreshold exceptions (<£189,000) would be retrospectively reported to
RCC and ARC every two months. This will become a standing item at ARC
rather than an annual report. ARC will review this position within 6 months.
2. Above threshold risk exceptions (>£189,000) would continue to require prior
approval from Board
3. Above threshold Pipeline risks, sourcing options and recommendations,
progress reports for material procurements, and bi-annual strategic pipeline
report would continue to be reported to the Board.
Prior to March 2020, subthreshold exceptions were approved by the relevant GE
member and SLP; and the Procurement Director. These exceptions were logged, with
mitigation plans to bring into compliance where exceptions were made for an ongoing
requirement rather than a one-off event. The Committee was asked what approach
they now preferred for the subthreshold exceptions given the ARC approved
approach. The Committee discussed the approaches including: RCC approval,
continuing with GE, SLP and Procurement Director approval and GE approval.
Ultimately, it was felt that the ARC approved approach was to instil more proactive
and transparent behaviours and therefore having GE overnight would add value to
the approach.
Accordingly, the Committee:
- NOTED the that no Procurement Risk Exceptions have been submitted to the
Post Office Limited Board since September 2020 and the contracts in the
Procurement pipeline;
- APPROVED the approach to the cheque processing contract and the proposed
next steps as outlined, subject to this being included in the paper for the
Board;
- NOTED the alteration in governance approved by the ARC; and
- APPROVED that GE should approve any subthreshold risk exception requests
moving forwards at its monthly meetings.
Post meeting note: It was agreed with the Company Secretary that as the Board
report on Procurement would be near identical to the ARC report that the report would

POST OFFICE LIMITED
RISK & COMPLIANCE COMMITTEE REPORT

7 A Meeting
Title: Risk Report Date: 12 January 2021

¥ " Alisdair Cameron, Group Chief
Author: Mark Baldock, Head of Risk Sponsor: Finance Officer

Input Sought: Noting & Approval

The Risk & Compliance Committee (RCC) is asked to note the current status of key risks and
GRC implementation and approve draft proposals on the role of the Board and Audit, Risk &
Compliance Committee (ARC)/RCC with respect to oversight of Post Office risk management
for onward submission to the ARC.

Executive Summary

RCC is asked to
. note current progress on the design and build of a GRC tool

. note a new set of system supported GRC risk reports will be presented to ARC in
3/2021.

. note the key risks we currently face with particular focus on those in the Commercial,
Financial, Operational, People and Strategic space

. approve draft proposals on how best the roles of the Board and the ARC/RCC with
regard to risk management should be clarified, aligned and are mutually supportive

Confidential

22 of 183 Post Office Limited - Risk and Compliance Committee-12/01/21

POL-BSFF-0238512_0021
POL00423694

POL00423694
Tab 3.1 Risk
Report
. What are our key risks (new and existing) and their materiality?
¢ How should our risk profile be governed? 3.1

Background

In 9/2020 we updated the way in which it reported to RCC/ARC on the status of the
Post Office’s key risks. We introduced a new Dashboard format designed to not only
provide a summary view of the Post Office’s overall risk profile but also a bottom up
analysis which showed how the lowest level (local) risks related to the next level
(intermediate) which, in turn, linked to the highest level (enterprise). At the same time,
we began to manage this dataset through RSA Archer. We saw this as a first step in
moving the away from a manual (very resource intensive) risk management process to
one which would become increasingly automated.

2. Much progress has been made but challenges still remain particularly around our recent
inability to rollout out the capability to manage risks to individual business units. Given
this, work commenced on the design and build of a Governance, Risk and Compliance
(GRC) capability to provide a structured corporate approach to governance, enterprise
risk management and regulatory compliance.

3. Phase 1 went live in 1/2021. This involved migrating the Central Risk dataset from RSA
Archer to Service Now (IRMPro module). Phase 2 (which is subject to separate approval)
will extend this risk management capability team to allow the business to manage their
risks via the on-line GRC tool rather than through the Central Risk team. Phase 2 will
also involve the migration of the IT and Finance Controls from Traction. This is targeted
from the beginning of 2021/22.

4. ARC have welcomed these developments but asked that future risk reports provide a
particular focus on the key risks faced by the organisation as well as a summary of the
risks across the 3 levels (enterprise, intermediate and local).

5. In doing so RCC are asked to note we have been unable, on this occasion, to provide
system-aggregated Dashboards etc as we are just finalising the transition from RSA
Archer to ServiceNow (IRMPro). This is scheduled to be completed by the end of 1/2021.
A refreshed set of GRC risk reports will be presented to ARC in 3/2021.

6. This paper also provides some thoughts on which types of risks should be discussed at
Board and/or ARC.

Key Risks

RCC will recall the recently approved Risk Policy places our risks into 14 themes. These
are broadly in line with those advocated by HM Government's ‘Orange Book’.!

8. We consider the key risks we face (new & existing, top-down & bottom-up are primarily
in 5 of the 14 themes namely Commercial, Financial, Operational, People and
Strategic. We will now look at each of these in turn. A complete list of our key

intermediate risks (rated 12 and above) across all 14 themes is provided as an
appendix.

* HM Government: Management of Risk ~ Principles and Concepts (June 2019)

Confidential

Post Office Limited - Risk and Compliance Committee-12/01/2 23 of 183

POL-BSFF-0238512_0022
POL00423694
POL00423694

Tab 3.1 Risk

Commercial

9. These are risks associated with the management of commercial partnerships, supply
chains and contractual requirements. Failure to manage such risks proactively could 3.1
mean the Post Office could suffer from poor performance, inefficiency, poor value for
money, fraud, and/or fail to meet business requirements/objectives.

10. A key Post Office risk in this area is around the management of our commercial
relationship with Fujitsu (4:4) for the remaining 3 years of the contract. Failure to
manage this relationship effectively will not only adversely impact the delivery of the
Post Office’s Telco strategy but also the timely implementation of Belfast Exit, SPM and
PCI compliance. To date, Fujitsu have resisted changes that could adversely impact
their financial position or deliver positive change in capability at speed and with control.
There is also the possibility Fujitsu may adopt an increasingly defensive commercial
position given the ongoing post-GLO Judicial Inquiry.

IRRELEVANT

IRRELEVANT
IRRELEVANT
i IRRELEVANT I In the event either of these risks materialise Post Office
performance service affordability could be undermined which will have a long term
adverse impact on the delivery of Post Office's purpose.

12. There are also commercial risks around adverse trading performance in part
prompted by the ongoing consequences of the pandemic. For example, there is risk
that travel insurance remains significantly impacted (4:4). Although POI have
successfully launched a COVID compliant product (and a travel product is back on sale)
ongoing and unpredictable European travel restrictions is likely to continue to hamper
travel recovery well into 2021. This will impact on POI recovery causing a negative
impact on delivering business objectives and/or on commercial viability.

Financial

13. These are risks arising from a potential inability to managing finances in
accordance with requirements and financial constraints. If such risks materialise the
Post Office could see a poor return on investments, a failure to manage assets/liabilities
or to obtain value for money from the resources deployed, and/or non-compliant
financial reporting.

14. At this point the Post Office face a number of risks around insufficient funding such
as not being provided with sufficient working capital, Network Subsidy, and/or
Investment Funding (all of which are currently rated as 4:4). There are also risks Post
Office is not provided with sufficient funding for the Historic Short Scheme compensation
(4:4) and/or for post-GLO criminal case compensation (4:4).

15. In terms of mitigating such risks clearly imminent decisions on working capital facility,

funding the HSS scheme will collapse.

Operational
16. These are risks that would arise from potentially inadequate, poorly designed or
ineffective/inefficient internal processes resulting in fraud, error, impaired customer 3.1

17. Potentially ineffective operational delivery manifests itself as a risk that Post Office
is unable to satisfactorily resolve Agent Postmaster recruitment and retention at
‘Hard to Place’ Branches (4:3).

19. Notwithstanding such risks evidence shows that Postmasters have continued to trade
strongly during the ongoing difficult trading conditions (particularly around Mails) and
have not reacted strongly to the GLO judgements. It is also the case improvements
have been made in the fairness and transparency of processes and improved
remuneration.

20. Our sense is that our COVID-19 response (efficient and effective PPE deployment and
enhanced remuneration) has been welcomed by Postmasters. Announcements of

Nonetheless, challenges remain including maintaining and enhancing Postmaster

21. Another key operational risk is around the potential financial fragility of multiple
partners (3:3). To help mitigate this risk arrangements have been put in place for
the regular financial monitoring of large multiple partners to pick up early warning signs.
In addition, work continues on the development and delivery of a refreshed network
strategy coupled with the delivery of new flexible & attractive propositions (including
increased automation).

22. Finally, the Safety Board have recently discussed a potential increased risk of physical
crime by external parties (4:3). This has prompted, in part, by very high levels of
cash being deposited at a small number of branches. To mitigate this risk, we are
reducing the amount of cash in branches with highest holdings, increasing the number
of cash collections at specific locations and introducing security deterrents and
protection including body armour and cash destruction (i.e. glue and ink capability).

People

23. These concern risks of potential ineffective leadership and engagement, a sub-optimal
business culture, inappropriate behaviours, the unavailability of sufficient capacity and
capability, industrial action and/or non-compliance with relevant employment
legislation/HR policies resulting in negative impact on performance.

24. In this area Post Office clearly face potential risks around an inadequate Work-Life
balance. The pressure on Post Office Central teams is large and increasing (4:4)

and ongoing and protracted home-working arrangements could have an increasingly

recent Post Office organisational structural changes has increased the risk of material 3.1

25. In part such risks are a direct consequence of continuing pressure to downsize the

and is complicated by job uncertainty, the pain of GLO findings and the particular
pressures of home working.

26. It is also the case that in the event Post Office does not proactively engage with the
CWU there is a risk of prolonged industrial action (3:4) which would adversely
impact our ability to deliver our short, medium or long-term strategic objectives
resulting loss in revenue, client/customer detriment and reputational damage. There is
growing concern that some form of industrial action will take place due to the planned
reduction of the DMBs. In terms of mitigation a dispute resolution has been put in place
(including GOLD teams) and relevant stakeholders have been engaged.

Strategic

27. These concern risks arising from pursuing a strategy which is subsequently seen as
poorly defined, and/or is based on flawed or inaccurate data or fails to support the
delivery of commitments, plans or objectives due to a changing macro-environment.

28. In this context the Post Office, like most companies, is subject to adverse external
economic factors. There is risk therefore we are unable to react quickly to fluctuations
in the economy that increase or decrease demand for our products and services (4:4).
Clearly at the moment there is greater economic uncertainty than normal due to COVID-
19 and Brexit. Indeed, this uncertainty is so widespread that most commercial
organisations are re-assessing their operations, viability and approach, which may
create an (unforeseen) secondary impact. It is also the case that Government economic
policy is stressed and its overall direction is unclear. Economic changes are already and
are likely to create both opportunities and risks as markets, competitors and partners
change.

Risk Governance
29. The Post Office Risk Policy is clear the organisation’s risk governance consists of the:

. Board: They are informed and advised by ARC. The Board have overall
accountability for the assessment and management of risk, taking a strategic view
of the risks faced by Post Office;

. ARC/RCC: They support the Board in its assessment and management of risks.
The ARC reviews Post Office’s risk policy, risk appetite and attitude to risk to ensure
these are appropriately defined and communicated so that parameters and
expectations are understood; and,

. Group Executive (GE): The GE, led by the Chief Executive, has operational
responsibility for Post Office risk management and systems of internal control.

30. This structure is underpinned by a standard ‘3 lines of defence’ risk model whereby the
GE and Post Office Groups (and their subsidiary business units) perform the 1* line

POST OFFICE LIMITED
RISK & COMPLIANCE COMMITTEE REPORT

7 Corporate Legal & Compliance Risk I Meeting
Title: Appetites: An Assessment Date: 12 January 2021
Jonathan Hill, Director of
Author: Compliance, Sarah Gray, Legal I Sponsor: Ben Foat, Group General Counsel
Director

Input Sought: Approval

The Risk & Compliance Committee (RCC) is asked to approve the corporate Legal &
Compliance Risk Appetite Statements for onward submission to the Audit, Risk & Compliance
Committee (ARC).

Executive Summary

RCC is asked to note:

. the paper applies the previously ARC approved Risk Appetite methodology to the
corporate perspective of legal & compliance risks across Post Office.

. In doing so, this paper notes the risk appetite of legal and regulatory issues are
managed through the three lines of defence compliance model. Generally, the LCG
function provides, amongst other things, 2" line of defence for legal and regulatory risk
management. This is not therefore a paper on the risks within the LCG function per se
but rather Legal and Compliance risks as they apply across the organisation. So include,
for example, the consequences of non-conformance with contractual obligations,
statutory requirements, regulatory requirements, corporate compliance (including
control effectiveness), and disputes/litigation. Once approved the appetites will apply
to all risks in this space wherever they sit in the business.

. the risks have been grouped into 6 themes (Contract & Transaction Management
Obligations, Statutory & Regulatory requirements, Disputes & Litigation, Employment,
Pensions & Associated Disputes, Corporate Compliance & Control Environment and
Intellectual Property & Brand). The Disputes & Litigation appetite excludes post-GLO
activity and Starling which are reported to the Board separately. We consider the
attitude to risks around the Historical Shortfalls Scheme merits a specific appetite
assessment. This is being pursued.

. work is being finalised on the supporting Key Risk Indicators (KRIs). We have already

identified an extensive range, but further work is required to complete the activity. This
will be done imminently and, by itself, should not prevent approval of these statements.

Report

What is the Post Office's appetite to corporate Legal & Compliance risks?

How are our current risks trending against such appetites?

What are we doing to bring any such risks outside of tolerance within appetite?
What are our proposed Next Steps and the timeline?

Background
In 11/2020 the ARC approved an updated Post Office Risk Policy which outlined an
a new approach to Risk Appetite such that we can meet our strategic objectives of (i)
supporting Post Office Postmasters, (ii) building a fit for purpose network and, (iii)
focusing relentlessly on Post Office core strengths and customers.

2. The Policy defines Risk Appetite as the amount of overall risk the Post Office is willing
to pursue (or retain) to achieve its strategic objectives. It also outlines a new 5-tiered
appetite scale (ranging from ‘Averse’ which borders on zero tolerance, through
‘Neutral’ to ‘Open’) against which individual risks will be assessed. In doing so the
Policy requires several factors to be considered including (i) our tolerance for
uncertainty ((ii) our decision choices and (ii) our strategic priorities.

3. The approved Risk Appetite scale is provided at Appendix A. Since approval we have
include some indicative risk rating ranges as a guide. These can be adjusted.

4. The General Counsel’s view is that the strict wording and definitions of Appendix A
(albeit a synthesis of generic industry-wide models) are on the more conservative or
cautious side of the classifications when benched-marked to other specific companies.
Depending on the Board's review of the application of Appendix A scale to the Legal &
Compliance risk appetite statements and assessment, it may choose to revise the scale.
For the purposes of this paper it has been adopted.

Lecal & Compliance Appetite
In parallel with Central Risk updating the Risk Policy and establishing a supporting
corporate approach to risk appetite the business has looked at how we can proactively
apply these tools and techniques to the corporate legal and compliance risks.

6. We have the grouped the 26 individual corporate Legal & Compliance intermediate
risks, which are set out in Appendix B, into 6 themes (Contract & Transaction

against this and what we have in train to bring any within tolerance where it is necessary
to do so.

Commentary
9. We havea Neutral appetite to being non-compliant with our contract and transaction

management obligations. This means, on balance, we will accept risks could materialise
here but only if their adverse impact is limited and are heavily outweighed by the
benefits. Active risks in this area will target a combined average impact and
likelihood risk rating of between 1-15.

10. At the moment we have one intermediate risk. This is around the potential failure to
manage our contractual obligations resulting in a risk of not realising assets and
commercial value (i.e. from the benefits under the contract (3:2).

11. This risk is in line with a Neutral appetite. so can be simply tolerated but monitored.
No further mitigations are required.

Statutory & Regulatory requirements Risk Appetite tracker

Commentary

12. Across this theme, in most circumstances, we broadly have an Averse appetite to being
non-compliant with our statutory and regulatory obligations (other than PCR). So, we
will avoid taking pretty much any risk in this area (where at all possible) and have an
extremely low appetite for any new risks materialising. In line with such an appetite
any active risks we manage will target a combined average impact and likelihood
risk rating of between 1 and 5.

13. Given the sectors in which we operate it is not surprising we have a significant number
of statutory and regulatory obligations, a number of which have associated risks
identified against them. These include potential non-compliance with new and
emerging regulation, an inability to meet evolving FS regulatory requirements,
adverse regulatory investigation, anti-money laundering non-compliance,
anti-bribery & corruption non-compliance, data protection non-compliance and
inadequate management of Whistleblowing incidents.

14. The majority of these risks have a risk rating of 5 or below which means they are in
line with an Averse appetite (but sometimes on the cusp) so no further mitigations
are required and they can be tolerated. However those risks on the outer limit of
tolerance will be carefully monitored to ensure they do not breach appetite.

15. Two risks (with an averse appetite) are currently breaching tolerance. These are

. Adverse regulatory investigation: if the Post Office engages in anti-competitive
behaviour, there is a risk that regulators or competitors could investigate and
successfully challenge such behaviour resulting in regulatory sanctions, penalties,
financial and reputational damage. This risk has a current rating of (5:2). We
consider this rating is merited given the high impact if Post Office engage in this
type of behaviour (e.g. fine of up to 10% turnover, third party damages, actions,
reputational damage etc). Mitigation measures are currently being worked on by
Legal to bring within risk appetite. This includes competition compliance training
being provided to Payzone and Legal teams from November 2020.

. Anti-Money Laundering non-compliance: If the Post Office fails to maintain AML
processes, policies and training there is a risk that it becomes non-compliant with
the requirements of anti-money laundering regulations. This risk has a current
rating of (3:2) so is just exceeding an averse tolerance. This score has been
reached primarily due to the risks associated with Fit & Proper, Premises

There are control remediation actions in place to improve the control strength and

These are covered in more detail in the MLRO Report.

16. It should also be noted we have a statutory and regulatory risk with a Cautious
Appetite. This concerns the risk of non-compliance with Public Procurement
Rules (PCR) which has a rating of (3:2) so is well within tolerance so can be simply
tolerated. We consider a cautious stance is appropriate here given the Board have
approved a PREN process which allows the approval (in limited instances) of direct
awards and PCR non-compliance.

17. That said the Procurement and Legal teams are working closely with the business to
increase PCR compliance and mandatory PCR compliance training has been rolled out
across the business from December 2020.

27. We have a Cautious appetite to risks around our Compliance and Control environment.

of non-compliance with Post Office's legal and regulatory requirements. At the

associated risks (and their impact and likelihood) in parallel with the analysis of the
effectiveness of the associated controls. A Cautious appetite will be applied once the
ratings are confirmed

Next Steps
31. The ARC are asked to approve that we should have
. an Averse appetite to risk of being non-compliant with our Statutory &
Regulatory requirements (excluding PCR) and Employment, Pensions &
Associated Disputes, (noting that we will be less averse to specific risks around
employee complaints and employment law claims)

. a Cautious appetite to risks around our Compliance and Control environment
and PCR in Procurement

. a Neutral appetite to Contract & Transaction Management Obligations,
Disputes & Litigation, and Intellectual Property & Brand)

32. The ARC are asked to approve that when viewed collectively the risk ratings within
each group are broadly in line with their associated appetite. There are a small number
of individual risks breaching tolerance which need to be managed accordingly and a
small number whose appetite has been flexed slightly, some of which are outside
tolerance.

33. The ARC are also asked to note that subject to approval we will ensure the target risk
ratings are fully aligned with the associated appetite. We are also finalising work on the
associated KRIs. We have already identified an extensive range and this work will be
completed imminently. This does not prevent approval of the appetites.

34. We will require risk owners of any corporate legal & compliance risk outside of appetite
(and wherever owned in the business) to proactively manage them to bring them within
tolerance.

35. Central Risk will provide assurance on such activity and will include an assessment of

risks against their approved Risk Appetite (along with the latest KRI trends) as part of
the bi-monthly RCC/ARC reporting.

‘ Tolerance for uncertainty: How willing is Post Office to accept uncertain outcomes? This illustrates the Board's appetite to trade off certainty to achieve a given
objective. A low rating demonstrates the Board's need for certain outcomes, while a high rating shows the Board will pursue an objective even with an uncertain outcome.

2 Decision choice: When faced with multiple decision options, how is Post Office willing to select a decision that puts a strategic objective at risk? This question assesses
the Board's acceptance that a given choice may lead to failure to meet a strategic objective. A Board who are averse will only choose options that pose a minimal threat
to the strategic objective's achievement. A Board open to this risk are willing to trade off the possibility of failure for a high-risk, high-reward decision.

> Prioritisation of strategic objective: How willing is Post Office willing to trade off this specific objective against achievement of other objectives? This demonstrates
the Board's willingness to pursue achievement of a given strategic objective over achievement of another. A Board who are averse to this would never trade off
completing the objective in question for failure of other objectives. A Board which is open would be willing to accept this trade-off.

Jonathan Hill, Director, Compliance Al Cameron, Group Chief Finance

Input Sought: Noting & Decision

The Committee is asked to:

° The Controls Framework update;
. The Data Management activities;
. Post Office’s approach to cookies;

approve the Data Governance recommendation to establish a Post Office-wide Data
Governance framework and SteerCo, based on the Digital Data Governance framework
but extended to encompass all forms of data;

approve the recommendation to assess the impact on Post Office's approach to cookies
of new European regulatory rulings against Google and Amazon, which may result in
changes to our cookie management and impact the business; and

Internal Audit programme and completion of audit actions.

for onward submission to the Audit, Risk & Compliance Committee (ARC).

on the latest internal audit position.

Tab 3.3 Compliance and Internal Audit Update
Compliance
3.3

1. The Controls Framework project has been established, initially, to review/identify gaps in
the controls established for the activities done to address the Group Litigation Order (GLO)
matters. We are working with the Historical Matters Unit (HMU) project teams, who are
identifying processes that have been updated or need updating following the Common
Issues judgement (CIJ), Horizon Issues judgement (HIJ) and the establishment of the
Stamps Scheme.

2. An interim report is expected before Christmas from KPMG relating to the HIJ, which the
Controls project will use as the basis to conduct controls reviews of the Horizon issues.

3. Plans are being developed with the HMU to review the actions taken to address the Stamps
issues, starting in January, which will include reviewing the associated controls.

4. Ways of working have been agreed between both HMU CIJ and Controls project teams
ensuring clarity of responsibilities and areas of focus. Weekly working group sessions are
in place to review progress to date, update on actions and make decisions.

Progress to date:

5. Recruitment of Controls Compliance Analysts.

6. The HMU team is mapping the processes and controls for Transaction Corrections, these
are being reviewed by the Control Framework analysts.

7. Changes to around 70% of processes have been made to the Onboarding of new
Postmasters, the Controls Framework project team is reviewing these to understand what
controls are in place and how effective they are

8. Designing and building a temporary Workflow on ‘Power Apps’ Tool to record controls
information against each business process, and facilitate assessment, assurance and MI
for the Controls Framework project

9. Testing the workflow tool and producing a training pack for the business outlining how
control owners and operators will need to support the project

10. Drafting a controls assurance template to support and facilitate the control assurance
work, ensuring design and operating effectiveness and consistency of Controls work;

11. A work plan for the Stamps Scheme and Controls review is being developed, with the
review planned for January and aiming to complete in February 2021, subject to Covid
restrictions.

Key issues:

12. There are various lists of actions taken to address the CIJ findings (e.g., the Norton Rose
Fulbright (“NRF”) review) but we are waiting on the HMU’s CIJ project pan to ensure we
have one, consolidated approach. In the meantime, activities are aligned to the NRF
review list of actions.

13. Initial controls reviews have identified that there is no consistent approach to mapping
the processes, if any, for activities addressing the CIJ remediation actions, which makes
it difficult to for the business to identify its controls and the project to provide assurance
that the controls exist and are effective.

Remediation activities:

14. In the absence of a consolidated list we are using the NRF review as the basis of our
controls review workstream

15. Where process maps are missing we are working with the HMU CIJ team to support the
creation of process maps

16. This may lead to re-work to assess the robustness of controls, where additional activities
are identified at a later date

Data Management 3.3

28. Increasingly management of data, in all formats, is a critical matter for all businesses.
Data governance in Post Office is managed through a number of different controls and
accountabilities. This is a decentralised approach and there is no single record depository
that enables the business to quickly identify and manage the data it holds.

29. Although there are a number of controls at the enterprise, group and function level (which
are set out through various policies e.g. Cyber & Information Security, Document
Retention & Disposal Policy, Protecting Personal Data etc), these are not well understood,
nor routinely adhered to.

30. It is recommended that regular monitoring and reporting against these controls through
the Risk & Compliance Committee (RCC) and ARC is required if Post Office is to successfully
embed them within its operations and manage its data. To enable this, it is recommended
that Post Office expands the remit of the Digital Data Governance framework and
associated SteerCo to cover all data, including physical documents and data stored in
external systems as well as the processes and procedures for managing it, with one
accountable member of Group Executive (GE).

Back office sites record assurance review:

44. The RCC instructed Compliance and Legal to instigate a search of all Post Office back office
locations to give assurances that all relevant information has been presented to Counsel
for Starling and PCDE purposes.

45. 93 locations were initially identified as being in scope.

. 44 were de-scoped as they have been determined to be ‘vacant’
. Vacant premises had to be cleared of all materials including records.
46. Of the 49 remaining locations the following can be reported:
* 33 sites confirmed there are no records or information stored onsite
. 13 confirmed that there are some boxes / files onsite. The highest volume is at the
‘corporate sites’ in particular Chesterfield and Swindon stock centre
. 3 are still outstanding, though are expected to be cleared by the time of the RCC.

47. The key challenges are:

. Determining if records located are relevant considering the broad scope of the legal
requirements (HSS, Starling, PCDE, Malicious Prosecutions etc)

. The mechanism for retrieval and scanning if required. We know from the Oasis
exercise that there is limited capacity to scan documents in a timely manner

48. Compliance and Legal are working on the next steps to ensure that all relevant
documentation is with external Counsel in time to meet 5‘ February disclosure
timeframes. Next steps include:

Record retention

49. The RCC Data Management paper identified that Post Office businesses needed to give
assurances that Record Retention Schedules, agreed as part of the Record Retention
Programme, were accurate and covered all processing activities.

50. All Data Owners were identified and provided with a copy of the Retention Schedules,
Remediation Logs and copies of the Document Retention and Disposal and Protecting
Personal Data policies.

51. Data Owners have expressed concerns with regards to the implementation of the
Remediation requirements. The key barriers reported back so far have been lack of
resource to implement the changes and the timeframes set for remediation to be
complete.

52. The responses from the data owners are being recorded and consolidated by the Chief
Information Security Officer (CISO) team as the owner of the record retention policy and
compliance is supporting the team. Going forward this should be coordinated through the
Data Governance framework.

Record management in branches

53. Compliance, Property Services and the Network team have stood up a mini-project to
implement a change programme for Records Management with the Branches. This project
will look to:

. Identify and communicate out to Branches what information needs to be archived
and preserved

. Create a new process for record archiving on a regular basis and not just on the
closure of a branch as is the process today

. Arrange for the necessary materials to be provided to branches to allow for archiving
to occur, including boxes, archive content labels and deletion dates

. Mechanisms for transferring boxes of records from branches to off-site storage
facilities.

54. The key challenges will be ensuring that;

. this project is sufficiently resourced to allow the new processes to be implemented,

. New processes are developed and shared with relevant stakeholders for consultation

. Communications and training materials created and shared across the Network to
enable a smooth transition into Business as Usual.

55. This work will commence in early 2021 as resources are currently focussed delivering
Christmas in branches and on the searches of back office locations.

Post Office Ltd approach to Cookies:

56. The CNIL (French Data Protection regulator) levied significant fines of €100m and €35m
respectively against Google (US parent organisation and Ireland subsidiary) and Amazon
Europe for breaching French Data Protection legislation with regards to their use of website
cookies under the ePrivacy directive. The ruling is not applicable in the UK. However, the
ICO is likely take into consideration the finding of the CNIL.

. Google - CNIL were clear that cookies are only lawful if informed consent has been
given. Consent should be requested using a banner on the user’s first site visit with
a clear link to how cookies can be disabled or opposed. Ability to refuse should be
offered in whole and part with information disaggregated by purpose. CNIL imposed

a deadline of 3 months to ensure individuals were adequately informed otherwise a
further penalty of €100k would be imposed for each day of delay.

57. Post Office continues to be ‘middle of the pack. We believe the Google ruling may have 3.3
an indirect bearing on our approach to cookies. In order to be compliant, it is
recommended that the following actions are undertaken.

. The Digital team and Compliance to carry out an assessment of the ruling and the
implications for Post Office and report back to the next RCC. The changes could
include:

. Default settings for all cookies except necessary to be switched to ‘off’ in
preference centre

. Persistent bookmark on all pages of Post Office website to allow users to re-access
the preference centre at any time

. Detailed information provided through cookie policy on vendors and duration

General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR) Contract Remediation

58. The Contract Remediation project was formally closed at the end of July as reported to
the previous RCC. Work is ongoing on and the number of outstanding contracts remains
at 7.

59. On all outstanding contracts GDPR clauses have been agreed by both parties and in two
(NCR and Santander) the eCAF process is progressing (the other parties are going internal
governance).

60. Fujitsu have agreed all GDPR clauses. The only outstanding issue is with one processing

Fujitsu Telecoms on agreement with the GDPR contract; it is aware of the significance and
are being chased for updates on progress

negotiations. This will continue until all outstanding contracts are finalised.

63. Overall completion of the annual ABC training was 98.4% as at 15th December 2020. The

Development are liaising with HR Business Partners to identify better controls for
contractors.

64. Submissions on the gifts and hospitality tool remain low due to Covid restrictions, however
a reminder was issued to colleagues in November due to a potential increase in gifts
leading up to Christmas.

Whistleblowing Update

65. All reports received in the last two months relate to the branch network, with the majority
received from agent assistants raising concerns about Postmasters and unethical
behaviour or conduct. Two reports received relating to a Postmaster subsequently
uncovered potential mails fraud that is estimated to be in the region of £500k. This is
currently being investigated by Post Office Security Operations, supported by the Royal
Mail Investigations team.

66. The contract to procure Protect (UK Whistleblowing Charity) to enable us to undertake
self-assessment and benchmarking is nearing completion and this should commence in
January. Protect have been asked to deliver tailored training for GE and Senior Managers
as early as possible in 2021 and we are awaiting their proposals.

79. As part of this consultation the government is considering imposing a single regulator to

government response (no date has been given for this) but we will need to understand
any potential regulatory risks or implications for Post Office if is decided that the cash
infrastructure should be regulated.

88. IT Control Framework (Ref.2020/21-13)

Needs Improvement

Sponsor:
Jeff Smyth

Audit actions:
i}

5
2
7

Appendix 5

The purpose of the audit was to assess how effectively the IT Control
Framework (ITCF) supports IT control assurance and risk mitigation
for POL, and whether it reflects the operation of IT related controls
across the business and provides accurate and timely information on
control performance.

We believe that the approach to implementing the ITCF is sound,
based on alignment with an internationally recognised governance
framework, with quarterly self-assessment and monthly reporting of
status and remediations. The 268 controls that make up the ITCF
and are largely aligned with what's happening on the ground and the
self-assessment process was generally operating effectively.

Additional work is required to ensure that the ITCF fully supports the
business. This should be addressed through migration to a more
comprehensive risk and control management tool, and the
introduction of strong second line activity to independently validate
the self-assessment process.

We conclude that the ITCF has continued to mature since its
implementation in 2018 and has further improved since our previous
review in 2019. However, we highlight that the operation of the ITCF
is not yet fully embedded within the business and was impacted by
the absence of a key staff member during April to July 2020.

Management Comment provided by Tony Jowett (CISO)

“Our IT controls framework allows us to better mitigate risks impacting POL’s IT. We have reached a
very good basic standard of maturity against COBIT 5 and other controls frameworks. This report
comes at a perfect time to help us move to the next level of controls maturity. I accept the findings in
it and will work to address them. Many thanks for the input.”

89. Mails & Parcels (Ref.2020/21-16)

The Mails & Parcels business is of key strategic importance to Post
Office, contributing one third of PO’s total revenue (approximately
£350m) in FY20. In Q3 FY21 Mails & Parcels contributed 63% of all
Network income.

The purpose of this audit was to review both the Customer and
Postmaster journeys for Mails and Parcels products, and to assess
the controls in place over related financial, operational and
compliance processes.

Appendix 7

Following the judgments from the Group Litigation Order, Post
Office has undertaken a programme of improvements to
overhaul culture, practices and procedures throughout every
part of the business. In addition to launching the Historical
Shortfall and Stamps Schemes, as part of its operational
improvement plan, and to address issues which arose from
group litigation concluded last year, Post Office has
established a new Historic Matters business unit (HM) to
oversee and deliver the programme of improvements.

Internal Audit is performing a review of the management and
oversight of the improvements delivery, as well as, the
appropriateness and sustainability of plans to address the
Common Issues Judgment (CIJ) and Horizon Issues Judgment
(HI3) findings. This is being done in three phases.

This second phase of the audit work assessed the programme
of measures that will oversee the delivery of the operational
improvements to address the criticisms from the CIJ.
Primarily, we sought to gain assurance that the operational
improvements recommended by Norton Rose Fullbright
(NRF), as a result of their analysis of the judgment, have been
identified and implemented in accordance with the planned
timescales.

We concluded that considerable work has been done to
address the judgment findings and implement the 34 NRF
recommendations. The status of completion of the
recommendations at the time of the audit report, is shown in
the chart to the left.

Progress is well evidenced throughout, although in some
instances, we have noted differences between the project's
view of progress, compared to that of Operations. There is an
Opportunity to address this by the adoption of a formal
handover process that clearly assigns the acceptance of
amended documents and procedures by BAU operations.

We highlight that further activity will be required within the
business to absorb the impact of the recent OE and the
premature departure of some key senior colleagues.

91. Belfast Exit Follow-up (Programme Assurance) (Ref.2020/21-11)

Needs Improvement

Sponsor:
Jeff Smyth

Audit actions:
i}

3
4
7

Appendix 8

Belfast Exit, also known as ‘Pivot 2 Cloud’, aims to migrate Horizon
- the combined group of applications that supports the Retail
business of Post Office - from the Belfast data centres (managed
by Fujitsu) onto a new cloud platform. The programme was last
assessed by IA as part of the 2018/19 plan, with a follow-up review
agreed as part of Internal Audit’s approved 2020/21 plan.

The objective of this follow-up review was to gain assurance that
the revised programme set-up, governance and delivery processes
are fit for purpose and operating effectively to enable the
envisioned benefits of migrating to the cloud.

Since its restart, the programme has focused on ways to reduce
complexity, add scale and delivery throughput, seeking
opportunities to add contingency and reduce delivery risks.
However, despite the good work done by the team, the technical
challenges and the risk of in-flight delays remain significant, as is
the potential future cost of failing to address some of the technical
challenges faced (technical debt), in order to focus on optimising
delivery.

We have rated the programme delivery as Needs Improvement,
however, we highlight that there is a significant residual risk
that is outside of the control of the programme. This risk
relates to the fixed deadline of exiting the datacentre when the
current lease expires. Management is currently assessing
contingency arrangements, with the most likely scenario being a
lease extension at an approximate additional cost of £4m. As a
result, benefits originally forecasted for FY22 and FY23 may not be
achieved.

92. PCI Compliance (Programme Assurance) (Ref.2020/21-17)

Needs Improvement

Sponsor:
Jeff Smyth

Audit actions:
i‘)

2
1
3

Appendix 9

The PCI compliance programme is a key compliance risk mitigation
programme for Post Office with a high level of risk, complexity and
cost. The implications and the level of changes needed to processes
and systems supporting both Payment and Banking Services, have
led to multiple programme iterations with several business case
revisions.

The programme was last assessed by Internal Audit as part of the
2019/20 plan and a follow-up review agreed as part of Internal
Audit’s 2020/21 plan. The objective of this follow-up review was to
gain assurance that the revised programme set-up, governance
and delivery processes are fit for purpose and operating effectively.

Significant improvements have been made to the programme since
the 2019/20 assessment. In addition, the degree of confidence over
the programme's ability to deliver has increased, with the
programme highlighting its underlying assumptions and risks in the
recent funding submission.

Despite the good work done, key challenges remain in the delivery
of a PCI compliant environment. This is primarily a consequence of
the inherent complexity of the chosen solutions, which are partly
bespoke and the heavy reliance on third parties.

There is a risk of additional costs and further delays in completing
the programme, which is not completely within Post Office control,
as the current delivery approach is governed by a Time and
Materials (T&M) contract with Fujitsu (with Ingenico operating as a
subcontractor of Fujitsu).

We note that the programme is still facing challenges in the level
of transparency and ease of engagement at functional level with
Fujitsu and Ingenico.

POST OFFICE LIMITED
RISK & COMPLIANCE COMMITTEE REPORT

Title: PCI DSS Compliance Meeting Date: I 2h January 2021

Joseph Moussalli, PCI DSS Jeff Smyth, Group Chief
Programme Manager Information Officer

Author:

Sponsor:

Input Sought: Noting

The Committee is asked to note:
« What progress has been made during the last reporting period?
e What are the key risks?

Previous Governance Oversight

The Risk & Compliance Committee (RCC) has requested a rolling update on PCI-DSS
programme progress.

Executive Summary

The PCI programme has made good progress in several key technical areas, with various
milestone having been completed. However, at the time of submission there are two main areas
for concern that the programme team is working through. The first is a recent discussion with
Santander around their delivery dates which may impact the programme timescales. The
second is an ongoing concern around risks associated with programme costs.

The programme consists of 2 core delivery streams:

1. The Point-to-Point Encryption (P2PE) workstream, which encrypts retail and banking
transactions from the Pin Entry Device (PED) to a PCI compliant zone in Ingenico before
onward processing to Global Payments (retail transactions) or VocaLink (banking
transactions).

2. The Target Operating Model (TOM) workstream which addresses use of PCI data by POL
in processes outside of the transactions occurring at the PED.

Retail Payments Pilot: During the last period we have implemented the new Ingenico Retail
Payments solution in five branches as a limited scale pilot. We are successfully taking Retail
payments and settling these transactions with Global Payments. This pilot was delayed by three
weeks due to a late defect identified by Fujitsu and caution from Post Office operations due to
an unrelated P1 counter issue that was happening at the same time. However, overall, the
team has delivered a release of good quality. Initial feedback from the trial is positive, feedback
is being collected and this will be factored into the Payment and Banking release.

Banking development: Significant progress has been made by Ingenico in respect of its
readiness for Vocalink accreditation, Ingenico has started testing with Vocalink and they remain
on track for 21 January 2021. However, the Pilot for Retail Payment and Banking transactions
has been impacted due to the significant effort to bring the Retail Payments Pilot (above) into
readiness. Fujitsu is indicating a three-week impact to the Pilot readiness date which is now
targeted at 26 March 2021 (5 Mar 2021).

The focus of the Target Operating Model workstream is to review the Products and

application that will be hosted in AWS.

digits. The Post Office team has worked with the business to adjust banking products

2. What are the key risks & issues on the programme?

The following are identified as key risks:

Risk: There is a high risk and thai IRRELEVANT cannot migrate services to route through
Vocalink within the timescales and this will significantly programme.

Mitigation: Post Office team is working closely with ‘3 to produce proposals
including costs and timescales. as allocated resources to scope the work but

this is running at a slow pace. The Post Office Director of Banking Services has been mad:

aware of the issue and escalation meetings are taking place. Jan 21 updat T
indicating that they cannot implement the required change unti A director level
meeting is scheduled for the first week of January to see if this delay can be mitigated.

Risk: Due to the complexity of the solution further funds will be needed. Fujitsu is
forecasting an overspend; further funds will also be needed to accommodate the change
in delivery approach. The contract is T&M and there is a risk of further overspend.
Mitigation: Post Office has challenged the forecast overspend with Fujitsu and will
continue to track and manage this issue. Further funding will be needed. Jan 21 update:
A change request has been raised to cover the additional funding for the known costs.

Risk: There is a risk that any changes needed to the Fujitsu/Ingenico software will impact
the plan. Fujitsu and Ingenico have given a commitment to meeting the current timescales
on the basis of no further changes.

Mitigation: Post Office has managed to descope some of the changes identified by
working with the business. At steerco it was agreed to implement two of the essential
changes - BIN>6 processing workaround and Ingenico tokenisation. TMC could not be
accommodated and so this will be delivered as a separate release. The steerco decision
aims to keep the implementation of the P2PE solution for banking on track but there will
be a delay to the PCI DSS compliance completion.

Risk: Bank of Ireland / Instant Saver. Due to the limitation in the Ingenico product to be
able to process BIN ranges greater than 6 digits we have had to ask Bank of Ireland to
implement a £10k deposit limit in their systems. There is a high risk that they cannot
implement this within our timescales which would delay the overall programme.
Mitigation: Post Office is working with BoI/IS to plan the migration. Early information
indicates that their development may not be complete until June 2021 at the earliest which
would directly impact the plan.

Risk: There is a risk that Coronavirus may impact the delivery timescales for any supplier
across the entire PCI programme.

Mitigation: Post Office is working with all engaged suppliers to better understand their
contingency plans to ensure that delivery momentum is maintained. This includes the
examination of options to minimise delivery impact by understanding key delivery person
risks, supply chain risks and other indirect or latent dependency factors.

Tab 4.2 Cyber Security
Crown Jewels
3. Acrown jewel is a system or system that:
a. Supports major revenue streams e.g., Horizon.
b. Supports our ability to function effectively as a business, e.g., Office 365.
c. Supports our purpose of being there in person.
d. Any outage or other event would cause adverse media interest. 4.2

72 0}

83

4. The aim of identifying our crown jewels is to provide a risk-based focus for
a. Targeting investment for maintenance and development.
b. Improving cyber defences and monitoring.
c. IT and management controls improvement.

5. The requirement to identify them and approve them at RCC level was a requirement from
the last Deloitte Cyber Maturity Audit.

6. The key roles associated with each crown jewel are:

a. System Platform Owner (SPO) - meaning the person that is responsible for
maintaining the IT capability of a system. This role is responsible for planning,
maintenance, operations, and through-life management of the platform. This is
normally a single person.

b. Business Data Owner (BDO) - the person or persons responsible for the
stewardship of the data on the platform. There can be several people who have
this responsibility for a system — especially in cases where multiple tenants use
the platform. An example of this is Service NOW which has BDOs across IT,
Compliance, Cyber Security and Branch Hub. A key requirement for the BDO is
to interpret the data retention requirements for the data they steward using the
POL Data Retention Policy as guidance. BDOs are also responsible for data
access recertification to ensure that only those who need data have access to it.

7. A list of our crown jewels along with SPO and BDO names is included at Appendix 1.

Report : What is the impact of the Solar Winds Attack on POL?
What has happened ?

8. Solar Winds are a global vendor of IT management software. They are based in the USA
with over 3200 employees world-wide serving 320,000 customers in 190+ countries. They
are rated number 1 in Network Management by analysts IDC. They are a major player in
the technology supply chain.

9. During September 2019 there was a breach of the integrity of the Solar Winds patch process
whereby a piece of malware was installed within a software patch for their Orion monitoring

product. At this stage the source has yet to be attributed but NCSC state that this is almost
certainly a nation state.

Confidential

Post Office Limited - Risk and Compliance Committee-12/01/2

POL-BSFF-0238512_0071
POL00423694
POL00423694

Tab 4.2 Cyber Security

@

10. That patch was then issued to customers starting in March 2020 with 18,000 Orion
customers receiving the patch worldwide. There is therefore the potential for widespread
infection as the patches are applied to customer networks.

11. On 8 December 2020 FireEye, a US-based Cyber Red Team and Incident Response
organisation, publicly announced that they had a major breach associated with the Solar 4.2
Winds patch where the attacker stole IP including red-team toolkits. Since then FireEye .
have been working closely with intelligence organisations to assess the extent of the breach.
Collectively they have uncovered a widespread campaign that includes victims in USA,

Europe, Asia and the Middle East.

12. The attack is currently active in other organisations. NCSC state they have no evidence of
impact in the UK but they are working with Government, Critical National Infrastructure
and Health sectors where there is known usage of Solar Winds products.

13. FireEye have provided details of technical countermeasures and indicators of compromise
for Security Operations Centres (SOCs) to enable them to deal with any infection.

What we have done in response to this attack?

14. We have confirmed with our major third parties that there is no exposure to POL assets as
they do not use Solar Winds products on our account. We will continue to work with them
to monitor the threat and stay alert to any impact on Post Office.

15. We have confirmed that we do not use Solar Winds products internally and have confirmed
this to BEIS.

16. We are providing assurances in turn to our business customers on demand.

17. We have contacted out Cyber Threat Intelligence (CTI) provider who are monitoring the
attack for any impact to us.

18. Our SOC has the indicators of compromise that would allow it to spot any infection within
the monitored estate and are in contact with our 3" parties SOCs.

What does this mean for us and what do we need to do now?

19. Whilst there is no known impact to us from this specific attack there may be more like this
to come. Sophisticated attackers that are well-resourced will always have the upper hand
when it comes to these kinds of attacks - they just have to get it right once whereas we
only need to get it right every time.

20. It is also worth noting that this attack may not have been aimed at one organisation
specifically - given the attack method involved then it is more likely that an attacker was

using it to generate a range of possible openings. For us this means that, as in many
scenarios, we may not be the target but we could become a victim inadvertently.

23. We will provide details of the measures we are proposing in the next paper but they will
include the more widespread use of drills and exercises across ourselves and our third
parties and the need for an incident response reserve pool that can be deployed rapidly to
get skilled people in place to get back to normal rapidly after any attack.

Joiners process, following on from the previous report’s focus on Movers. Our analysis covers

operating model and complete the action from the recent audit to develop a roadmap to get us
