Questions addressed
qd. What are the complexities that we need to address associated with the Joiners process?
Qi How well have managed those complexities during the period September-November
2020?
3. What are the GLO IT team doing around JML in Horizon?
4. What steps are we taking to improve JML and produce a cohesive plan and roadmap? 4.3
Report
a In this report we are focusing in on the Joiners process for staff and contractors. This

follows on from our work in previous reports covering Movers and Leavers.

What are the complexities that we need to address associated with the Joiners
process & how well have we managed them?

2a Effectively joining a role has the following key risks, and issues with associated controls:

a. People join without background checks being completed, which could
mean individuals with adverse financial, employment or criminal records join the
Post Office. This is mitigated through HR processes - the issuance of a contract
is subject to vetting. Therefore, this is unlikely to materialise.

b. People join and do not have IT kit or access available on day one,
meaning that they cannot work or seek to work from their own home kit and
email addresses introducing data leakage issues.

c. The way in which kit is provided seems haphazard, meaning that on joining
people receive upwards of 6 separate couriers delivering components of their IT
kit. Our equipment comes from a range of suppliers so whilst this may be
inconvenient, we cannot change this in the short term. There is also a concern
that personal information is held by more couriers than is ideal although all of
our couriers are bound by data protection legislation and our standard
contractual terms.

d. People join and do not have building access - this has not been analysed
due to the low numbers of people requiring access and the additional special
arrangements in place for such people.

e. People join and do not get paid - this is not a cyber-related risk as such.
Also, this tends to be resolved quickly by the line manager and new staff member
when they realise that they have no pay at the end of the month. This risk has
not occurred at POL for a number of years due to effective process.

3. Further analysis has been performed on 2.b above for the 42 joiners during the period
September to November 2020. All users had their accounts enabled and used from day

one. There were some joiners who were contractors joining as permanent staff who
already had an account that was useable.

4. For 2.c above then the provision of the kit was reliant on line management ordering it

4.3

5. In summary, of the three areas in JML then Joiners appears to be the least risky and the
most under control.

What are the additional measures the GLO IT Team have put in place around JML in
Horizon?

6. A significant part of the IT estate in POL is run by 3" parties and this is the case for
Horizon. Here, the governance of JML is reliant upon attestation through monthly
security management meetings with Fujitsu at which they state they have (or have not)
met the required security standards across a range of measures including JML and
Privileged Access Management.

7. In the current round of Organisation Development we are proposing to increase the size
of the cyber compliance team so that it has the capacity to perform additional and deeper
data sampling and audits that will serve to increase the assurance around JML.

8. Horizon requires several internal and external parties to maintain the system which is
open to errors and mis-communication. Therefore the GLO IT Team have focused on:
a. Achieving good process governance.
b. Understanding and defining expected behaviours of the different team roles
associated with the JML process.
c. Using assurance to confirm that our third parties our following our framework
and identify any process shortfalls

9. The proposed GLO IT Action plan is shown in Appendix A.
10. Key focus areas in the plan include:
a. Introducing a culture of awareness as roles are defined across divisions will
essential.
b. New responsibilities will need to be introduced with the introduction of data and

system owners. Ensuring we take a pragmatic approach so that our
requirements are met with understanding and not a simple box ticking approach.

fils We will report the progress of this as plans unfold in future papers.

. Tony Jowett, Chief Information .
Author: Security Officer Sponsor: Jeff Smyth, Group CIO

Input Sought: Noting

The Committee is asked to note the status and plans regarding the reduction of risk associated
with IT Controls for onward submission to the Audit, Risk & Compliance Committee (ARC).

Previous Governance Oversight
Request from Chair of ARC for update at previous ARC meeting.

Executive Summary

We have run formal IT Controls since 2017 using the COBITS framework. Every quarter we
assess ourselves on those controls and where necessary perform targeted remediation
activities, reporting through the IT Leadership Board.

A series of audits have been conducted by Internal Audit with the latest rated as “Needs
Improvement”. This, combined with our own analysis of areas to focus on and the changes
made by recent organisation development activities, will form the action plan for improvements
during 2021.

Questions addressed

1. What have we done to date on IT Controls?
2. What is our current status and what are our improvement activities?

Report: What have we done to date on IT Controls?

1. A previous paper in March 2020 provides a detailed view of our controls framework - the
highlights are summarised below.

2. The Post Office Limited (POL) IT Controls Framework (ITCF) is based on the internationally
recognised COBIT5 framework. The ITCF was put in place during 2017 following a detailed
review and recommendations from KPMG.

3. The ITCF has 268 controls across 17 COBITS processes that were selected as being the
most relevant to POL and the risks being faced at the time of inception.

4. The ITCF provides an ongoing assessment of the operation of controls and any outstanding
remediations for control deficiencies, based on quarterly control self-assessments (CSA).

Confidential

84 of 183 Post Office Limited - Risk and Compliance Committee-12/01/21

POL-BSFF-0238512_0083
POL00423694
POL00423694

Tab 5 IT Controls

@

5. CSA activity and remediations are tracked using PWC’s Traction toolkit. Each CSA is
triggered and managed by Cyber Security. The results, escalations and remediation
activity raised by the CSA process are reported to the IT Leadership Board (ITLB) for
further action.

6. The results from the most recent CSA are included at Appendix 1. These show that 83%
of controls are operating effectively. Of the remainder, 10% are not operating effectively
and 7% are not being operated due reliance on key people who are absent or have left.
For the non-effective and not operated controls there are remediation plans in place with
progress being monitored monthly at ITLB.

7. Other control frameworks in use include those for specialist areas such as Cyber Security
and Service Management - the assessments from these are fed into the ITCF which sits
above all of these.

8. On wider engagement initial meetings have been held with the POL-wide project team on
controls to ensure that there is alignment between it and the ITCF.

Report : What is our current status and what are our improvement
activities?

9. To understand whether we are doing the right things in the right way Internal Audit have
reviewed the ITCF:
a. During May 2019.
b. As part of the maintaining minimum control standards review in August 2020.
c. In December 2020 - the report for this is included by IA at this committee.

10. Internal Audit have concluded the following:

a. “Significant progress has been made following the implementation of the ITCF
in 2018 and the Internal Audit review in 2019, with greater clarity around control
operation, increased senior management and control owner engagement,
improvements to the TrAction tool that supports the framework, generally
increased self-assessment completion rates and enhanced tracking of
remediations.

However, operation of the ITCF has been impacted by key staff absence and
support should be provided to minimise this risk in future. Management should
continue to refine the design and operation of the ITCF to ensure that control
weaknesses are quickly identified and addressed, supported by migration to
ServiceNow and robust assurance of control design and operation.

The ITCF continues to mature but is not yet fully embedded within the business
and, therefore, we have rated this report as ‘Needs Improvement’.”

12. In addition to the audit IT have conducted their own assessment of control areas that
need further investigation as to their fitness to the current environment based on the risk
to our crown jewel assets.

13. Firstly, in response to the work conducted under OD we need to review that we have the
correct control ownership across all COBIT 5 processes.

14. IT Controls exist to manage risk. Therefore we are focusing our improvement effort on
COBIT 5 controls of greatest risk, namely those areas connected with the management of
our 3" party estate through the lens of our crown jewel systems.

15. We will assess whether the controls are appropriate, at the requisite maturity level and
have the right coverage. The COBIT5 processes to be focused on are:

a. Manage Suppliers - starting with coverage of our suppliers running crown
jewel systems.

b. Manage Service Agreements - specifically obligation/SLA checking/ making
sure we meet business need and define future business needs for inclusion into
contracts from a controls perspective.

c. Manage Continuity - as an area that has changed under OD and also has had
key-person risk.

d. Manage Portfolio - ensuring that we understand the resource constraints on
some of our 3" parties and manage the risks accordingly with major projects
and programmes in those areas.

e. Manage Enterprise Architecture - ensure we have coherence across the
estate whilst we go through the necessary changes from major programmes
such as Belfast Exit, PCIDSS, EUC and SPM.

16. The above areas will be reviewed internally by the POL controls team using guidance
available within the COBIT5 documentation set.

Tab 6 Supply Chain His! al IT Risks
POST OFFICE LIMITED
RISK & COMPLIANCE COMMITTEE REPORT
Title: Supply Chain Bolton IT Risk Update I Meeting Date: I 12 January 2021
Ruther: Russell Hancock, Supply Chain Sponsor: Al Cameron, Group Chief
Director Finance Officer

Input Sought: Noting
The Committee asked to note the issues highlighted, the steps being taken to identify and
reduce any potential risks and to agree on next steps and further updates.

Background

As part of the preparation of moving off of the Bark Street Bolton site the IT team were asked
to assist the Supply Chain National Cash Account Reconciliation Team (NCCART) on moving
some bespoke hardware to the new Bolton site.

The original system was installed as a key part of the closure of Manchester Cash Centre in
2010 with the NCCART team being relocated into Bark Street. The entire relocation appears to
have been managed centrally as a property team project with the CCTV relocation
subcontracted the Kings Security. It would appear that at no point was the central Post Office
IT consulted or advised as part of this project.

IT colleagues discovered that the NCCART team were using a PC in order to access the CCTV
systems across the SC cash centre estate, primarily for the purpose of viewing camera footage
in relation to disputed branch discrepancy claims. The system gave the user open access to
download or view in real time the live CCTV feeds both internally and around building
perimeters. This open access link had the potential to make all cash centre locations vulnerable
to a planned robbery through being able to identify weak spots in building perimeters, ascertain
when maximum cash is exposed and potentially identify colleagues in order to coerce through
Tiger Kidnap activity.

The PC concerned also had the following issues when IT team investigated further: -

- No anti-virus running leaving the machine vulnerable to viruses

- No internet security so nothing stopping unauthorised connections or spyware getting
access

- No data encryption on hard drive

- Running a non-enterprise operating system meaning the system is never patched or
fixed

- No domain so no central management of the PC

- Memory sticks being used to transfer data with no encryption as no Bitlocker on PC.

- Open access to the internet.

Once these issues were identified all of the PCs were removed from site including the memory
sticks and CD's and transferred to secure storage at Manchester CVIT depot.

Strictly Confidential

The machines have now been transferred into the custody of KPMG who are currently
undertaking a technical forensic examination in order to determine if the machines have been
compromised in any way. As of Monday 4" January 2021, their findings to date match the
business purpose of the PCs and portable media and there is no evidence to suggest that the
PCs and media have been used in any other way or in a way that exposes POL to undue risk. The
full examination and report will be published w/c 12" January 2021.

In order to give wider reassurance the IT team were then asked to undertake an audit of all SC
sites to ensure that all IT equipment contained within was compliant and posing no risk to PO
IT infrastructure, identifiable to IT team and logged on their inventory of equipment and up to
date with latest protocols. At present this audit is hallway through completion but the team
have identified the following points to be noted: -

ital CCTV Systems

dycams & BT Routers

Bo

With over half of all SC sites having now been taken through a thorough on site audit, apart
from the unique Bolton risk and the CCTV risk identified above, no other threats or issues have
become apparent and I have been assured that Supply Systems are fully compliant.

How did we get to this position?

All of the at risks systems were updated some time ago with the most recent change being the
NCCART migration into Bark Street, Bolton and the provision of body worn cameras by CVIT
crews.

Without exception all of the at risk equipment identified by IT colleagues had been installed as
part of one off bespoke project, managed by either property or security on behalf of the wider
business. These projects were completed at a time when Post Office didn’t have the level of

change governance that exists today, the activity at the time being managed as a single
enclosed project.

As part of our ongoing deep dive we've not be able to find any handover into BAU IT ownership,
so this has meant that this particular infrastructure has been sitting outside of IT governance
and ownership. We have also struggled to find background data and decision paperwork to
identify the methodology used to sign off, fund and implement the programmes that delivered
these projects.

As a result of the above, the equipment was installed, tested and once complete, the project
team disbanded handing over to BAU teams. Those teams have then just carried on using the
systems and tools given to them, assuming that all was correct and configured appropriately
as it had been installed by business contractors.

When subsequent IT equipment upgrades have taken place, these machines have been
bypassed as they are not included on the fixed asset register of IT equipment. Regrettably none
of the operational teams ever challenged that these machines never seemed to have oversight
or checking over the course of time.

Next Steps

- Continue the audit of Supply Chain sites concluding in Jan 2021

- Expand the scope of internal Audit to include areas of Supply Chain that have not been
traditionally covered (such as cash management team)

- Ensure that audit process is sufficiently varied throughout the year to enable a wider
inspection of all aspects of Supply Chain operations and support.

- Creation of an annual Supply Chain online support course that highlights awareness of
ensuring process and operating practices are fit for purpose, how to call out when they’re
not, building upon lessons learnt from audit programme.

- Report back to RCC in March with a further, final update.

Sally Smith, Money Laundering

Title: Meeting Date: I 12 January 2021

Input Sought: Discussion & Approval
The Committee is asked to:

- review and discuss the annual report and its conclusions as part of its role in monitoring
the adequacy and effectiveness of the Group’s anti-money-laundering systems and
controls which ensures Post Office’s meets its regulatory obligations under the Money
Laundering Regulations; and

- approve the recommendations within paragraphs 9 - 25 of this report and paragraph F
of the Annual Report of the Money Laundering Reporting Officer, prior to the Annual
Report being issued to our regulator, HMRC;

for onward submission to the Audit, Risk & Compliance Committee (ARC).

Previous Governance Oversight

January 2020 Annual MLRO Report and updates via the Risk & Compliance Report at the Risk
& Compliance Committee (RCC) and ARC.

Executive Summary

The Money Laundering Regulations (MLRs) require that the Money Laundering Reporting Officer
(MLRO) produces an annual report to appraise senior management on the effectiveness of key
Anti-Money Laundering and Counter Terrorist Financing (AML/CTF) controls, and make
appropriate recommendations for improving the management of risks, priorities and resources,
if appropriate.

Questions addressed

1. Is Post Office complying with the requirements of the regulation?

2. What are the key AML and CTF risks within Post Office Ltd and are there any significant
gaps or weaknesses in the Post Office’s compliance with its regulatory obligations under
the Money Laundering Regulations (MLRs)?

3. What are the key activities that need to be undertaken to address these gaps?

Report

4. The full annual report is attached and covers the key reportable regulatory responsibilities

under the MLRs. Appendices referred to in the report are in the reading room.

CONFIDENTIAL

92 of 183 Post Office Limited - Risk and Compliance Committee-12/01/21

POL-BSFF-0238512_0091
POL00423694
POL00423694

Tab 7 Money Laundering Annual Repor

Financial Impact

5. Post Office have not received any regulatory penalties since 2017/2018 when HMRC fined
us c.£1.1m for regulatory breaches in relation to Travel Money. During 2020, Post Office
self-reported a regulatory breach to HMRC in respect of 6 branches that had traded Bureau
de Change despite not being registered. HMRC advised they would take no action or
sanction against Post Office, but confirmed that they will judge all matters on their individual
circumstances and therefore any repeated breaches may result in sanctions.

6. We continue to see increasing scrutiny by regulators in the UK, reflecting the guidance in
the last Financial Action Taskforce Mutual Evaluation Review. This year fines of c.£84m
have been levied by UK AML regulators, including c.£307k by HMRC.

7. HMRC annual registration fees were c.£1.7m in 2020 and additional costs are associated
with Fit & Proper requirements and transaction monitoring and assurance.

Risk Assessment, Mitigations & Legal Implications

8. Products and services provided by Post Office are broadly in line with the risk appetites set
by the Board although there continues to be a significant increase in potential money
laundering via Banking Framework cash deposit services. There has been an improvement
in residual risk over the last 12 months across most products and services.

Conclusions and Recommendations

9. The regulatory environment continues to pose a challenge, with increased regulatory and
legislative focus on money laundering and terrorist financing. The 2020 National Risk
Assessment (NRA) published in December 2020 demonstrates risks remain high, particularly
in relation to cash based services, and calls out in the executive summary the risks
associated with banking cash deposits over Post Office counters. We expect HMT to
introduce an Economic Crime Levy in 2021, which will increase the regulatory fee burden
for Post Office and increase scrutiny from law enforcement and supervisors as funding from
the levy is applied to target money laundering risks. Risks will continue to be monitored
by the MLRO, providing regular updates and reporting to the RCC and ARC, and
sponsorship of required actions will be required by GE.

10. Covid-19 has had a significant impact on the Financial Crime landscape, and there has been
a general increase in frauds, scams and changes in Organised Criminal Gang’s (OCGs)
activities. There has been an increase in scams relating to vulnerable customers and we
have seen examples of suspicious activity in the Post Office related to Bounce Back Loan
(BBL) fraud. The volume of SARs has doubled and investigations have risen by a third in
the last 12 months, with no indication that volumes are reducing. The additional PO
grade recruited into the Financial Crime Compliance team in October and funded
until the end of 2020/21 will need to be fully funded for 2021/22.

11. During the year Amazon vouchers were increasingly targeted by fraudsters scamming
vulnerable customers. Work has been undertaken to identify tighter controls to mitigate
the risks around this Post Office product, however, the Product Manager is a Payzone Bill
Payments employee and does not have access to Post Office tools and change procedures,
which has meant some delays to required changes. The Bill Payments/Payzone teams
need to identify and implement a solution to this issue, to ensure that the product
managers have access to the tools and systems to manage Post Office products.

12. There has been significant focus over the last 12 months by the National Economic Crime
Centre (NECC) and Post Office in addressing the risks relating to cash deposit services, and
Post Office needs to be able to demonstrate that it is both aware of the risks and has

controls in place to manage them. In 2020 up to P8 there have been investigations into c.
£198m suspicious cash deposits — more than double that for the whole of 2019. We have
continued to support the NECC Project Admiralty established to tackle the cash deposit
threat over Post Office counters and established a Banking Framework Services AML Sub
Group to highlight and discuss the issues with the banks.

13. Cash deposits completed in 2020 relating to Money Transmission Businesses have
highlighted an unforeseen issue with businesses that deposit excessive volumes of cash
through a single location. Whilst actions have been taken to address the physical security
risks associated with these, further consideration is needed to tackle the money laundering
placement risks associated with accepting high value cash deposits from high risk
businesses. Robust solutions need to be identified and put in place by the Product
Team, with the support of Compliance to ensure that Post Office can demonstrate
to regulators, if called upon to do so, that sufficient controls are in place.

14. The HMRC supervisor who had overseen Post Office regulated activity since 2015 retired in
May 2020 following handover to a new supervisor. The new supervisor secured a new role
in the summer and a further supervisor was appointed in August. Due to Covid, HMRC are
not undertaking any supervisory visits or meetings, and it is unclear what level of scrutiny
we may see in 2021. Weare also expecting a further consultation on HMRC’s fee structure
in 2021.

15. Training is a key AML control for Post Office and there have been some instances of
poor/late completion of mandatory training by employees and contractors and we still see
instances where SARs should have been raised by branches. The resource responsible for
training, awareness and communications was transferred to the Communications Team in
March 2020, but continued to deliver all aspects of the role. However, the role was removed
in November 2020 as a result of OE, and whilst the Communications Team will still support
communication activity, the work to support training content will have to be undertaken
primarily by Financial Crime Compliance moving forward, and this may impact the ability
to further enhance training. Compliance will monitor training completion more
closely in 2021 and further awareness raising via Area Managers will be
undertaken.

16. Both first and second line management have responsibility to ensure that the controls in
place work as intended. Quarterly assurance checks against the policy minimum control
standards by Financial Crime Compliance evidence that a number of first line controls are
only partially effective. The planned activity in 2021 by Compliance on the Controls
Framework should help improve first line understanding and application of their
responsibilities.

17. Products and services provided by Post Office are broadly in line with the risk appetites set
by the Board and there has been a general improvement in residual risk over the last 12
months. There have been delays starting the Payzone financial crime risk assessment due
to resourcing and funding challenges, but these were resolved in Q3, and recruitment of a
fixed term contract Financial Crime Manager to complete the work is progressing. The
Payzone assessment is scheduled to be delivered at the end of the financial year.

18.A SAR received from one branch identified a complex trading structure with links to
overseas money transmission companies via the Bill Payment Services, exposing an issue
relating to product understanding of exactly what types of businesses are serviced under
re-seller contracts. The controls relating to Re-seller contracts will be reviewed as
part of the Payzone and Bill Payments reviews in 2021.

19. The accredited FIO within Security Operations who assisted with the review of SAR
disclosures relating to possible Post Office employees and postmasters has left the business
and the replacement resource will not have FIO accreditation. Financial Crime
Compliance will monitor the impact of this additional workload moving forward.

20. The Post Office PCI DSS Compliance project will impact Financial Crime Compliance adding
additional time to some activity, as card data will need to be accessed, processed and stored
in a ‘clean room’ environment. Additionally, during the rollout period, both the existing and
new systems will need to be accessed when undertaking investigation work. Financial
Crime Compliance continue to work closely with the project team, and have called
out that additional PO resource may need to be funded by the project to support
the transition period.

21. As in 2019, significant challenges with the Fit & Proper project have continued with
considerable oversight and support required. The new system for generating annual
declarations, capturing data and generating the required reporting for Post Office internal
governance and HMRC agent data provision was delivered in July 2020, but there are on-
going data integrity issues (resulting on one occasion in requesting from HMRC approval of
a late submission of monthly data).The F&P team has moved under a new team leader as
a result of OE, therefore the knowledge and expertise built up by the original team leader
during the last 6 months will be lost. Further oversight and work will be required to
ensure compliance in 2021, and Internal Audit have been asked to schedule a
review of regulatory data requirements for both Fit & Proper and Premises
Registration.

22. Despite improvements relating to direct employee F&P processes implemented in the first
half of 2020, the most recent OE changes have made it difficult to keep this up to date as
individuals leave the business and role titles/responsibilities and line management changes
have occurred. Financial Crime Compliance will continue to work closely with HR
to resolve current discrepancies and implement more robust controls.

23. Further work has been undertaken to improve and implement Bureau de Change monitoring
reports which has led to an increase in cases, despite the reduction in overall transaction
volumes caused by Covid. The new reports have enabled the identification of potential
vulnerable customers and highlighted customers travelling to multiple branches to purchase
currency, further evidencing the improvements in monitoring controls since the HMRC audit
in 2016. There continue to be issues with data and reporting around potential Sanctions
matches, and whilst there are manual workarounds, Accenture have not yet fixed the
underlying issue. Monthly meetings now held with the Travel Money team ensuring they
have direct oversight of the activities to support their product, and also ensuring Financial
Crime Compliance are kept up to date with any product changes or developments.
Accenture and the Data Centre of Excellence need to identify and implement a fix
to the Sanctions data issue. The effectiveness of Bureau de Change monitoring
reports will continue to be reviewed by Financial Crime Compliance.

24.In summary, the framework of AML/CTF controls is generally effective and Post Office is
meeting its regulatory requirements under the MLRs. However, there are key areas where
focus needs to be maintained to ensure this continues, particularly in relation to:

i. The general increase in frauds, scams and changes in Organised Criminal Gangs (OCG)
activities across industry caused by Covid and the ongoing economic outfall

A. Purpose and Scope of Report

1. As required by the Money Laundering Regulations (MLRs) this report appraises senior
management on the effectiveness of key Anti-Money Laundering and Counter Terrorist
Financing (AML/CTF) controls and their application, and makes recommendations for

improving the management of risks, priorities and resources.

2. HMRC is the regulator responsible for supervising Post Office Limited (POL) compliance
with MLR requirements. Their oversight relates to POL Money Service Business (MSB)

activity, specifically, the provision of Bureau de Change.

3. Organisations are required to adopt a risk-based approach to prevent money laundering
and terrorist financing. Risk assessment must be documented and evidence the decisions
that senior management have made in the context of the particular risks facing the

business.

4. POLis required, both contractually and under the MLRs, to have in place and comply with
policies, controls and procedures to mitigate and manage effectively the risks of money
laundering and terrorist financing for all the products and services it offers through third
party or white label solutions and joint venture arrangements (e.g. MoneyGram, Banking

Framework Services, financial services products, Gift Cards, etc.).

5. The most significant impact of financial crime for Post Office continues to be reputational
damage. Negative media attention following an incident has potential for loss of consumer
and client confidence in the product/service, consequential devaluation of brand values
and possible impact on Government commitment which is vital to support Post Office.

6. The MLRO and the Financial Crime Compliance team are responsible for financial crime
policies, assurance and AML/CTF risk assessment of products and services (see Section C
and Appendix B).

7. Post Office have not received any regulatory penalties since 2017/2018 when HMRC fined
us £796,500 in respect of premises registration errors, and £344,766 for regulatory
breaches in relation to oversight and risk assessment of Travel Money, but we continue to
see increasing scrutiny by regulators in the UK, and c.£84m of fines have been levied on
firms by UK regulators in 2020, including c.£307k by HMRC (see Appendix C for summary
of UK enforcement action)

8. Post Office Insurance is subject to a separate MLRO annual report each year, which
can be found in Appendix A.

9. Ben Foat is the GE member and officer appointed to be responsible for overseeing
compliance with the MLRs.

10. Sally Smith is the MLRO and a member of the Post Office Compliance team and is ordinarily
located in Finsbury Dials, Moorgate, London, where Post Office Group is situated. The
MLRO takes ultimate responsibility for compliance with the MLRs, the provision of training
and awareness within Post Office, the design and implementation of internal anti-money
laundering policy, systems and procedures, and advising on how to proceed once an
internal report and/or Suspicious Activity Report (SAR) has been made.

11. Under the direction of the MLRO, POL’s exposure to financial crime is assessed and
assured. This includes

« setting policies and standards relating to financial crime, assessing and assuring
AML/CTF risks across Post Office;

«ensuring that risks are properly reflected in Risk & Controls Matrices (RACMs);

products and services.

outcomes of risk assessment work, HMRC supervisory activity, changes to legislation and
industry issues.

16. See Section B above for summary of governance and oversight.

ii. Staff awareness and training

18. Awareness and training is a key control for Post Office, and annual training was updated
to cover issues and incidents that had arisen in the previous 12 months. All staff are
required to complete AML/CTF training:

« Back office employees must complete within 30 days of joining and annually
e Customer facing employees and agent assistants must complete before they have
access to Horizon and annually

19. Monitoring of training completion levels for back office staff is undertaken by HR Directors,
and those staff who do not complete mandatory compliance training are dealt with via
conduct with potential removal of annual bonus payment. Training completion is subject
to quarterly assurance checks by Compliance. During 2020, there have been a number
of occasions when completion rates have been below 95%, primarily due to non-
completion by contractors, although there have been a couple of individuals who have
failed to complete compliance training. These individuals have been chased by Learning
& Development and new controls are being identified for contractors to ensure timely
completion.

20. Annual training was completed between ist and 26'" May 2020, although due to Covid,
branches were given an extension until 14" July 2020 to complete the training.

21. We have been working closely with Area Managers to raise awareness of emerging trends
and issues, and the higher volume of SARs received from the network and CViT in 2020,
seems to indicate that key areas of training are being understood and acted upon,
although there are still occasions where SARs are not being raised.

22. 29 branch and business awareness communications on AML, Financial Crime and SAR
reporting have been delivered in 2020, including a number of communications highlighting
vulnerable customer scams. The overall volume of communications reduced in 2020 (from
51 in 2019), partly due to consciously reducing branch impacts during Covid, but also as
a result of greater liaison with Area Managers to raise awareness of particular non-
conformance issues and scams.

iii. Risk assessment, policies, controls and procedures

23. The Group takes its legal and regulatory responsibilities seriously and consequently has*

e Tolerant risk appetite for Legal and Regulatory risk in those limited circumstances
where there are significant conflicting imperatives between conformance and
commercial practicality

e Averse risk appetite for ligation in relation to Financial Services matters

e verse risk appetite for not complying with law and regulations or deviation from
business’ conduct standards for financial crime to occur within any part of the
organisation

e Averse risk appetite in relation to unethical behaviour by our staff.

24. The Group acknowledges however that in certain scenarios even after extensive controls
have been implemented a product or transaction may still sit outside the agreed Risk
Appetite. There were 3 risk exceptions relating to financial crime opened in 2019 and
closed in 2020. These were relatively low risk and there was only 1 new risk exception
opened in 2020, and this related to Payzone terminals in POL branches (see Appendix D
for details).

25. All new products and services have been subject to risk assessment before go live, and
existing products and services have been subject to annual re-assessment as required.
(see Appendix B for details).

26. Three Post Office Insurance products that were overdue for assessment in 2019 as the
Product Owners returned the Product Information Packs late (Gadget, Motorbike and Pet
insurance) were completed in 2020.

27. Policies relating to Financial Crime overall and AML/CTF specifically, have been updated
and were approved at the July 2020 Audit and Risk Committee, and published on the
Intranet via a One Communication.

28. Both first and second line management have responsibility to ensure that the controls in
place work as intended, and the Financial Crime team undertake quarterly assurance
checks against the minimum control standards. No controls have been assessed as
ineffective, however, there are some areas that are only partially effective and there is
on-going work to improve these controls. MI on minimum control standards has been
improved and is reported each month. Now that the Fit & Proper system and process has
been delivered, quarterly assurance checks for the Fit & Proper Policy Standard will
commence in January 2021.

29. We review, investigate and report all instances of non-conformance with AML policies and
processes, ensuring corrective action is taken by relevant business owners.

30. Processes within Compliance are robust and up to date, however processes and policies
across the business to support these are less mature and continue to require
improvement. The planned Compliance Controls Framework will assist with improving
business processes and first line compliance with minimum control standards.

v. High risk products and services

35. Post Office branch pre-order and on-demand Bureau de Change represents the highest
direct risk for POL in terms of AML/CTF and regulatory compliance. Oversight and
monitoring enhancements in 2020 include:

« Improvements to the existing monitoring reports to enable:

o identification of low value suspicious linked transactions
o customers/branches who have processed high volumes of declined transactions

o identification of potentially vulnerable young customers following cases identified
at the beginning of March

¢ Amonthly MI dashboard on monitoring, investigations and SAR activity which is
shared with the Travel Money team, together with a monthly compliance meeting
to review the MI and discuss any issues identified.

36. There are ongoing issues with the Bureau de Change Credence universe delivered in 2018.
Accenture rectified the issue concerning Sanctions whereby customer information was not
being pulled through for transactions that declined due to a Sanction match. However,
during live testing it was identified that not all transactions processed by a customer are
flowing through. There have been ongoing discussions between the Data Centre of
Excellence (DCoE) team and Accenture but a solution to rectify has not yet been identified.
DCoE believe that this is a defect in Accenture’s original design and the original logic to
link transactions is incorrect. Weekly calls between the teams to identify a solution are
ongoing.

37. During the first Covid lockdown, Financial Crime Compliance supported numerous business
projects to adapt to the challenges and ensure that POL was able to support our
Postmasters and customers. These included on-boarding Partner Banks to the Payout
Now service and simplifying the application process for Drop & Go.

38. Other products that are high risk include:

e Banking cash deposits - We have seen a rise in cash deposits from Partner Banks via
POL counters as more customers become aware of the services and more bank branches
close. The Covid pandemic and lockdown restrictions throughout 2020 contributed to
this and there has been a rise in suspicious activity reports. Financial Crime Compliance
have identified third party deposits, money-mules and suspicious deposits by Money
Service Business agents. Over the last 12 months there has been a significant rise in
investigations and SARs relating to cash deposit services (see paras 46 & 76).

e The UK Financial Intelligence Unit (UKFIU) published a report in 2019 calling out the
increased number of Post Office cash deposit SARs received, the lack of visibility POL
has over bank's customers (meaning that SARs lack detail that would assist Law
Enforcement) and stating there were clear indications of money laundering at some POL
branches through these services. As a result, Project Admiralty and the SCGC AML Sub-
group were established at the end of 2019 to tackle the money laundering risk posed
to POL and the banks. This has led to positive changes by some banks to help reduce
the risk. This includes reviewing their customer deposit limits, implementing more
timely monitoring of POL transactions, and Financial Crime Compliance sharing weekly
reports of POL branches investigated for high value deposits.

e Following the Banking Framework Services (BFS) risk assessment in 2019, Financial
Crime Compliance have been working with the Banking team to mitigate the risks
identified. This included reviewing the BFS agreement wording in relation to the roles
and responsibilities of each party. With the aim to make the agreement clearer, new
proposed wording was produced by Legal and presented to the SCGC AML Sub Group
members in July. Following verbal feedback, amendments were drafted by Legal and
circulated in December. The banks have been asked to provide written feedback before
31%t January 2021, so that these can be addressed before the proposed wording is
formally submitted.

e MoneyGram - As a near real time money transmission service, MoneyGram remains
high risk for laundering and scams and there is significant training and awareness
activity to help front line staff identify issues. MoneyGram is consistently one of the top
three products for which we receive suspicious activity reports by the network each
month (see para 75 & Appendix E).

e Gift Cards - Due to the anonymous nature of the product, we continue to see criminal
activity which is addressed through training and awareness activity. In response to the
Fifth Money Laundering Directive the One4All gift card limit was reduced to £120.
Blackhawk (who now own Gift Cards) has approached POL to implement further controls
to mitigate the risk of financial crime, and Financial Crime Compliance are supporting
the Product team to implement these controls.

e Amazon Vouchers - Between July and September, POL saw an increase in suspicious
activity reports relating to Amazon Voucher purchases (see para 77). In most reports
it is suspected that the customer is potentially vulnerable and has fallen victim to a
scam, and in some instances suffered substantial losses. Financial Crime Compliance
have been supporting the Product Manager to implement controls to reduce the risk.
The Product Manager is a Payzone Bill Payments employee and does not have access
to POL information available on the intranet, e.g. Group policies or change procedures.
This has caused difficulties in progressing change in a timely manner and meant that
we have not yet been able to reduce the risk to our Postmasters and customers.

e Bill Payments Reseller —- It was identified this year that an MSB was using POL bill
payment service, via Capita, to deposit cash relating to money transmission activity.
Deposits were between £10,000 to £20,000 and taking place on a daily basis at one

vi. Customer due diligence
39. POL is required to undertake customer due diligence for directly regulated activity (e.g.
Bureau de Change) when:

e Establishing a business relationship, or
e Carrying out an occasional transaction with a customer of €15,000 or more, or
e¢ Money laundering or terrorist financing is suspected

40. For Bureau de Change, the following controls are in place:

e Horizon restricts single or multiple transactions in the same basket to £10,000 (well
below the €15,000 occasional transaction limit).

e Training and Horizon prompts advise that no business transactions should be
undertaken and that customers should not transact more than £10,000 in any 90 day
period.

e Customer details and ID are taken for all transactions of £1,000 and above, and for all
transactions settled by card payment. Monitoring is undertaken to identify linked
transactions for the same customer in a 90 day period and corrective action is taken as
required (see para 74 re. Bureau de Change investigations).

e As part of POLs risk based approach, all transactions of £2000 and over are subject to
a real time eKYC, PEPs and Sanctions check, with real time declines of eKYC failures
and Sanctions matches.

e Horizon users are trained to decline transactions and complete a SAR if they suspect
money laundering or terrorist financing.

41. PEP matches are monitored post transaction with corrective action taken if necessary. We
have identified some matches over the last year, however, we have not identified any
concerns as the amounts transacted are very low.

42. Where there is a potential match on a Sanction list the transactions are declined in real
time. In 2019/20, there were 4 potential sanction matches and there have been 4 up to
P8 YTD 2020/21, which have resulted in further investigation. Following review, none of
these potential matches are the actual sanctioned individuals and therefore we have not
reported to the Office of Financial Sanctions Implementation (OFSI).

43. In 2019/20, transactions for 42 customers (£217,900) were prevented from breaching the
£10k over 90 day Bureau de Change threshold through monitoring activity. P8 YTD
2020/21, transactions for 25 customers (£116,267) have been prevented. We expect this

44. For all other products and services, POL is not directly responsible for customer due
diligence, however, there are some contractual obligations where part of customer due
diligence on behalf of the third party client or supplier is undertaken. For example, where
POL acts as agent for MoneyGram, we must comply with their policies and processes in
relation to recording customer data and identification details. As part of product and
service risk assessment work undertaken, the requirement for customer due diligence,
PEPs and Sanctions checks is considered, and where appropriate, work is undertaken with
the product manager to ensure the right controls are in place.

45. All SARs are investigated and, where appropriate, disclosed to the NCA by Financial Crime
Compliance. SARs received relating to third party clients are shared with them so that
they can conduct an investigation against their own KYC and transaction records.

46. We continue to see an increase of Cash & Valuables in Transit (CViT) drivers raising SARs
due to the volume of cash they are collecting. There was a spike in referrals during the
first Covid lockdown due to increasing levels of cash being accepted at some branches,
which seemed suspicious as most retailers, pubs and restaurants were closed and people
were being encouraged to pay by card to help reduce Covid infections. We believe that
part of this increase was due to banks opening on reduced hours or temporarily closing
during the height of the pandemic, but many are suspicious and we continue to see more
reports via CViT.

47. The volume of SARs received increased from an average of 312 per month in 2019/20 to
600 in 2020/21, and in September and October there were over 800. This increase of
c.90% was mainly driven by unusual activity identified from Bureau de Change monitoring
reports, complex banking investigations completed by Financial Crime Compliance, and
SARs received from the Network relating to suspicious bureau activity and banking cash
deposits (See Appendix E: Report on duties of nominated officer for additional
information).

48. The 2 accredited FIO’s within Financial Crime Compliance have reviewed, investigated and
responded to 145 SARs disclosed by the NCA in 2020, in comparison to 75 in 2019. Some
of these related to suspected Bounce Back Loan fraud by persons associated with POL
branches. An additional 21 SARs were investigated and responded to by an FIO within
Security Operations. This FIO has now left the business and Security Operations will not
be recruiting resource with FIO accreditation, therefore Financial Crime Compliance will
inherent this workload moving forward, and the impact will be monitored.

iii. Record keeping

All record keeping relating to AML/CTF is electronic (all paper SARs and paperwork are
scanned and saved electronically) and filed within restricted access folders, with a
retention schedule in place. As the team have been working from home due to Covid, a
workaround was put in place for paper SARs to ensure these were collected weekly from
Future Walk and returned for scanning on to the system and shredding.

50. All reports, risk assessments and supporting documents are filed in the AML Sharepoint
site, and a log is maintained to ensure annual review and sign-off.

51. For Bureau de Change, the AML Credence universe maintains all branch on-demand and
pre-order Bureau de Change transactions and this is retained for 5 years.

52. Financial Crime Compliance investigation cases are managed and recorded via Microsoft

ix. Premises Registration

55. Following the HMRC fee increase in 2019, the product team analysed non-profitable
branches and permanently removed the product from 3,257 branches. As a result of Covid,
discussions were held with HMRC to look at deferring or reducing the annual premises fee
due on 1% June 2020 as business volumes had dropped dramatically. A compromise was
reached whereby the annual fee was deferred to 1*t December 2020, and a further 2,679
branches were temporarily ‘paused’. It was agreed that fees for any that were switched
back on before 1** December 2020, would also be deferred. The annual fee subsequently
paid on 1%* December 2020 was £1.656m for 5,512 branches.

56. On 22nd October 2020, it was identified that some de-registered branches were able to
transact Travel Money. Subsequent investigations identified that 6 branches completed 25
transactions totalling £6,784.30 between the 1st June 2020 and 28th October 2020. These
6 branches were included in the permanent removal and pausing of HMRC branch
registrations at the end of May and should have had their functionality to transact Travel
Money “switched off”.

57. Historic inconsistencies were subsequently identified between Post Office Master Data
System (MDM) and Fujitsu RMD data records, causing the file transmitted to “switch off”
the transactions to be unsuccessful for these branches. It was also identified that a further
35 branch records relating to Travel Money transactions were inaccurate but did not result
in any transactions.

58. We advised HMRC verbally on 2.4 November 2020 of the issue and provided a written
report on the 6'* November 2020, detailing the anomalies and confirming that the 6
premises impacted were sent for registration on 2° November 2020, and that the relevant
agent (responsible persons) data was still held, they were still considered ‘Fit & Proper’
and would be supplied in the November monthly HMRC report.

59. HMRC advised on 13 November 2020 that they would be taking no further action or
sanction against POL, but confirmed that they will judge all matters on their individual
circumstances and therefore any repeated breaches may result in sanctions.

60. Fixes were identified and all the data mismatches were corrected week commencing 9"
November 2020, and controls are now in place to prevent future issues; updates to the
Fujitsu data records are automated and only transmitted via the strategic Post Office MDM.

for delivery in January 2021 in time for the next cohort. This will allow straightforward ‘no
change’ redeclarations to be completed via this simplified route.

D. Investigations and Incidents

71. There were 689 investigations up to P8 2020/21 (an average of 86 per month), compared
to 727 for the whole of 2019/20 (an average of 61 per month). Bureau de Change
investigations up to P8 2020/21 are almost at the level for the whole of 2019/20 and it is
expected this level will continue as enhanced monitoring reports are developed and
implemented. Monthly meetings are held with the Travel Money team to review MI and
trends, and determine if further system changes or controls are required in Horizon to
reduce manual investigation activity.

72. Banking investigation volumes to P8 have exceeded the total seen for the whole of
2019/20, mainly due to increased banking cash deposits due to Covid, bank closures and
reduced opening hours. Since March 2020, a growing trend was identified with MSBs
depositing significantly high volumes of suspicious cash over our counters (see para 76).

73. The graph below shows the investigations undertaken in 2019/20 and up to P8 2020/21
and is split out by the high risk products:

74, Bureau de Change (volume and value of branch transactions 2019/20 5.96m & £1.38bn,
and to P8 2020/21 914k & £241m).

¢ Due to Covid, transactions are significantly down compared to last year, although up to
P8 YTD 2020/21 there have been 556 investigations reflecting criminal and customer
behaviour changes. These related to branch non-conformance, money laundering and
confirmed card fraud.

e Refinement of monitoring reports has led to an increase in the identification of potential
vulnerable customers and helped highlight customers travelling to multiple branches to
purchase currency:

o A high number of potentially linked customers travelled excessive distances from
their home addresses undertaking multiple purchases of foreign currency, which
continued during November lockdown. This was referred to the NECC, due to
suspicions about links to Modern Slavery/Human Trafficking. The majority of the
customers ceased transacting following direct escalation to Regional and
Relationship Managers and contact with branches.

o An emerging pattern was identified, potentially linked to County Lines drug dealing,
involving several young males and females (16-22 years old) completing
transactions at multiple POL branches. A branch focus article was issued to the
Network and information shared with Regional and Area Managers to raise
awareness. The card information was shared directly with the card issuing bank,
and the information was referred to the NECC.

e All non-conformance is escalated to the Area Managers and Contracts teams, and there
have been a number of Area Manager visits and conversations in order to prevent any
further breaches. Up to P8 2020, 69 incidents were escalated to these teams.

e Via Microsoft Dynamics 365, a record is maintained of mitigation undertaken for each
case. Up to P8 2020/21 volumes of activities needed have increased significantly:

o 1480 Branch phone calls (1602 for whole year 2019/20)
o 287 Text blasts (121 for whole year 2019/20)

e Multiple examples have been identified in 2020 of customers sharing cards, travelling
to transact, and purchasing lower values possibly to avoid card limits or to obtain cash
advances. Where possible, transactional bureau information is shared with the card
issuing banks to aid their investigations. These bank referrals have helped limit further
breaches.

75. MoneyGram (volume and value 19/20: send transactions 2.5m & £561m, receive
transactions 340k & £99m. Volume and value to P8 19/20: send transactions 1.6m &
£416m, receive transactions 176k & £53m).

e Monthly meetings are held with the MoneyGram compliance team to review any issues.

e During 2020, the Network has reported vulnerable customers sending money to various
South American countries and India. Trends have been identified including fraudsters
targeting vulnerable customers posing as Microsoft requesting access to home
computers and HMRC demanding tax payments. All reports have been escalated to
MoneyGram via the fortnightly SAR update and urgently via phone if required.

e Concerns were raised directly to the MoneyGram Compliance Team regarding
individuals sending high values to Romania and Albania, using one branch to transact.
In some instances, these customers all claimed to live on the same streets. This resulted

in the suspension of the MoneyGram service at the branch and after review the branch

e MoneyGram have identified 52 potential branch non-conformance issues up to P8&

branch, either in person or over the phone, discussing the failings and providing further
training where required. Prior to each review, Financial Crime Compliance complete a
check of each branch to confirm whether any other concerns exist.

76. Banking Framework Services Cash Deposits (Volume and value 2019/20: 41.3m &
£22.1bn. Volume and value up to P8 2020/21: 25.5m & £12.5bn).

e There were 90 investigations totalling c.£198m relating to suspicious high volume/
value banking cash deposits up to P8 2020/21. This is compared to c.£98m across 80
investigations in the whole of 2019/20.

e The examples below have been identified from intelligence received and SARs raised by
branches, cash centre staff, Area Managers and third parties:

o There has been a significant increase in large cash deposits being made by MSBs
operating as sub-agents for Money Transfer Services across the UK, with the
majority in East London. At one branch in East London, c.£68.5m was deposited
between 12/02/2020 and 16/11/20 onto c.40 different bank cards and into multiple
different bank accounts which we understand all belong to MSBs. c.£9.9m has also
been deposited at a branch less than half a mile away with some individuals
depositing at both branches. Following referral by Financial Crime Compliance, one
bank has exited two MSB relationships, and one bank is writing to their MSB
customers to advise that they must pay in via the bank and not POL so they can
closely monitor activity.

o Intel from one bank identified, high value cash deposits being made onto multiple
banks cards. Between February 2020 and October 2020, c.£17.6m was deposited
at 148 different POL branches, predominately in the South, South West and South
East of London. Following law enforcement engagement, an individual was
arrested and pleaded guilty to money laundering offences

o SARs were received from two branches in South East London and Kent raising
concerns that customers were depositing large volumes and values of cash into two
different bank accounts. It was identified that deposits were taking place at 58 POL
branches totalling c.£3m between 03/08/20 and 24/09/20. Working closely with the
banks these accounts were exited and the activity subsequently stopped. Area
Managers were asked to speak to the impacted branches to ensure deposits are
refused if these customers return.

o A branch raised a SAR concerning high values of cash being deposited into a
Hydroponics business. Investigation identified four additional cards depositing at 4
other branches and between September 2019 and 23 March 2020, c.£4m was
deposited in total. Branches advised that the notes often smelt of cannabis and the
individuals provided contradictory information regarding their nature of business,
they also often deposited Scottish notes which was unusual for their area. After
liaising with law enforcement and the banks involved, a number of individuals have
been arrested on suspicion of drugs and money laundering offences.

77. Amazon Gift Vouchers - SARs received in the second half of 2020 highlighted customers
duped into purchasing vouchers and providing the voucher code to fraudsters over the
phone. Often the fraudster telephones the customer alleging to be from HMRC and

advising that they owe large amounts of tax which has to be paid that day otherwise they
will be arrested for tax fraud. The customer is told that the only way they can settle the
debt is by purchasing Amazon gift vouchers. In one example, an elderly customer visited
a branch on three occasions and purchased £14,500 worth of vouchers with cash
withdrawn from his bank, on each occasion he advised that he was buying these for friends
and family. The branch reported this to Grapevine and were advised to initiate Banking
Protocol should he return. He returned the following day and attempted to purchase a
further £6,600 worth of vouchers, Police were called and while the customer was waiting
to be served, he received a call from the fraudster asking if the transaction had been
completed. When the Police questioned the customer, he finally admitted that he was
buying the vouchers and providing their code numbers to someone claiming to be from
HMRC, as he had to pay a tax demand or he would be arrested. Further checks carried
out identified he had visited two other branches and purchased £30,000 in total.

Multiple nationwide communications to the network have been issued and awareness
raised via the weekly Retail Network. Financial Crime Compliance are working with the
product team to implement additional controls to reduce the risk (see para 38).

78. Card Fraud over POL counters - Between ist January 2020 and 30th November 2020
there were 2345 fraudulent card transactions processed totalling £202,592.81. In the
same period last year there were 2198 totalling £250,549.25. The majority of fraudulent
transactions over £100 continue to relate to Gift Card purchases. Investigations are
carried out where a particular branch is being targeted, or when high value amounts have
been purchased. Branch communications have been delivered and SARs raised where
required.

E. External Threats/Landscape
i. Business areas

80. There have been no significant changes to the regulatory landscape during 2020 as a result
of changes in the Post Office business model.

ii. The impact of Covid-19

81. Covid has had a significant impact on the Financial Crime landscape, and there has been a
general increase in frauds, scams and changes in Organised Criminal Gang’s (OCGs)
activities across the financial services industry both in the UK and overseas.

82. An increase in financial crime has been reported throughout 2020 due, with the NECC
reporting that they expected to see increases as typologies adapted to the new restrictions.
By April, Action Fraud had reported that 862 reports had been received relating to COVID
with losses totalling over £2m.

83. The Fraud Advisory Panel reported that the following Covid fraud risks had been identified:

e Phishing emails and letters (especially via Zoom and Microsoft 365).
« Fraudulent/fictitious investment opportunities advertised via social media platforms.
« Fraudulent coronavirus insurance adverts.
e Fake fines claiming the individual has broken lockdown rules.
« Business impersonation (e.g. requests for employees to update their bank details).
« Companies advertised on eBay potentially involved in Bounce Back Loan fraud.

POL-BSFF-0238512_0110

Tab 7 Money Laundering Annual Report

@

« Financial services firms seeing widespread evidence of government grants, particularly
Bounce Back Loans, being exploited with a high level of sophistication by both OCGs
and individuals.

« Fraudsters paying young adults for their personal details which are then used to form
companies, open bank accounts, and register for grants.

84. The NECC with support from UK Finance, established a Covid Fusion Cell at the beginning
of April, which ran until December 2020. It included banks, Post Office, UKF, CIFAS,
Insurance, IFB and British Telecom plus the public sector (NECC, HMRC, COLP, MPS) to
share information to identify changes in the economic crime threat and criminal behaviour
during the restrictions and target where action could be taken against criminal behaviour,
and identify alerts/typologies/communications to be issued cross-sector to help prevent
economic crime.

85. Post Office has seen an unprecedented increase in SAR reporting and investigations (see
paras 47 and Section D), and has also identified a number of existing or new agents via
SAR disclosures and agent applications that may have mis-used, or attempted to mis-use
Bounce Back Loan and other Government backed Covid support. We have also seen a rise
in vulnerable customer scams and frauds and a number of activities have been undertaken
to address these issues (see paras 38 & 74). Business Plans Analysts referred potential
mis-use of BBL funds in new Postmaster applications to Financial Crime Compliance who
sought guidance from the British Business Bank. This guidance was used to establish and
issue guidelines for the Agent On-boarding teams and Business Plans Analysts.

iii. Fifth Anti Money Laundering Directive

86. The Fifth Money Laundering Directive (SMLD) was implemented in the UK on 10th January
via the Money Laundering and Terrorist Financing (Amendment) Regulations 2019. There
was limited impact to Post Office in respect of the new regulations:

e Enhanced Due Diligence was expanded to include business relationships or transactions
involving high-risk third countries and required enhanced monitoring. MoneyGram
implemented changes to its processes for sending and receiving money involving high-
risk countries and these changes were communicated to branches.

e Electronic Money amendments reduced thresholds for anonymous pre-paid cards.
GiftCard limits had already been reduced to a maximum £120

e Risk assessments must be completed prior to the launch or use of new products,
business practices and delivery mechanisms. This is an existing minimum control
standard in POL policies, but first line adherence is inconsistent and steps have been
taken to address via training and awareness.

iv. National Risk Assessment (NRA) of Money Laundering and Terrorist Financing
2020

87. The third NRA was published on 17'* December 2020. It concludes that it remains difficult
to quantify the scale of the money laundering threat to the UK, but it is likely that levels
have increased since 2017 and estimates that serious and organised crime costs the UK
economy £37billion a year.

88. Key findings include:

e Traditional high-risk areas of money laundering remain, including financial services,
money service businesses, and cash. However, new methods continue to emerge within
these, as criminals adapt to increased restrictions and exploit vulnerabilities in different
sectors and emerging technology.

112 of 183 Post Office Limited - Risk and Compliance Committee-12/01/2

POL00423694

Tab 7 Money Laundering Annual Report

@

e Retail banking services continue to be assessed as high risk of being abused for money
laundering. Criminals continue to target retail bank accounts to facilitate the rapid
transfer and layering of criminal proceeds. Those that accept cash deposits face the
highest risk.

e Cash-based money laundering is still heavily characterised by the use of cash intensive
businesses to disguise criminal sources of wealth, or by smuggling large amounts out
of the UK. However, an increase in the abuse of cash-related services has been noted,
such as cash deposit services in Post Offices, and the use of cash couriers and CViT
companies. This is alongside continued abuse of legitimate UK services, such as money
transmission and retail banking.

e Currency exchange is stated to be usually lower risk than money transmission services.
This is due to the large volume of money that can transfer across borders and the
vulnerabilities associated with money transmission business reliance on a complex
network of agents and sub-agents to deliver their services enhancing the risk of
exploitation by criminals.

e  Itis estimated that at the end of 2019, there were around 4,800 criminal groups in the
UK, comprising approximately 37,000 associated nominals.

e NCAassesses it is likely fraud in the UK is increasing, with a considerable rise in reported
losses

e The threat to the UK from terrorism was raised to ‘severe’ (an attack is highly likely)
on 3 November 2020.

e The size of the illicit drugs market in the UK in 2018 was estimated to be £5.3 billion,
but recent research into specific drugs suggests the actual figure is much higher.

e¢  Detections of migrants attempting to enter the UK illegally have increased, and the
criminals facilitating them continue to use high-risk smuggling methods.

v. National Economic Crime Centre - Public/Private Initiatives

89. Following the establishment of the NECC in 2019, they are improving public-private
partnership by bringing together all public-private activity under one governance forum
through the creation of a new NECC Public Private Operational Board (PPOB). The working
groups within JMLIT have been reduced to 3 (Fraud, Money Laundering & Terrorist
Financing), with the agreement that ‘pop-up’ groups will be established where necessary
to tackle particular threat (e.g. the Covid Fusion cell set up in April and Project Admiralty
set up end 2019 to co-ordinate responses to the increased threat of cash deposits over POL
counters).

90. Since March, the JMLIT Operational Working Group has not been able to meet, as there is
not a safe alternative to the physical weekly meetings at the NCA, but matters have been
dealt with via secure correspondence and some group and bi-lateral conference calls.

91. Work has been undertaken during the year, and supported by Post Office, on the Public
Private Threat Update relating to MSBs and the 3'¢ National Risk Assessment on Money
Laundering and Terrorist Financing, published in December (see section i.v. above).

92. In the Autumn, HMT in issued a consultation on the proposed Economic Crime Levy. The
paper indicated that their preferred option is to have a revenue based levy with some
exemptions for small businesses where it is not cost effective to gather the levy. We have
put forward a position that as all POL regulatory revenue is derived via payment of dividend
and commission from FRES, who are also regulated and subject to the levy, this would
effectively be a ‘double-levy’ and therefore POL revenue should be exempt. We are unlikely
to see a response to the consultation, and the draft legislation until the new year.

vi. FCA penalty for Anti-Money Laundering failures

93. In June 2020, FCA fined Commerzbank AG (London Branch) £37.8m for failing to put
adequate AML systems and controls in place between 2012 and 2017. Commerzbank was
aware of these weaknesses, which FCA had previously raised with them, and had failed to
take reasonable and effective steps to fix it.

vii.

Sanctions Policy after 315t December 2020

94. When the UK's transition period after Brexit comes to an end, EU sanctions will no longer
apply in the UK. The UK sanctions regime will come into force, from 11pm on 31%t December
2020, under the Sanctions and Anti-Money Laundering Act 2018 (Sanctions Act). There will
be no gaps in implementing existing sanctions regimes after the transition period. Some
EU sanctions regimes may not be addressed through regulations under the Sanctions Act
by 31 December 2020, but these will continue as retained EU law under the EU (Withdrawal)
Act 2018. The government will implement UN sanctions in UK domestic law after the
transition period.

Other external threats

95. In February 2020, the NCA published an Amber Alert to warn registered money service
businesses that serious organised crime networks were specifically targeting the sector for
its weaknesses in detecting dirty cash. During lockdown Post Office identified an increase
in independent MSBs (or independent retailers registered as agents of larger companies,
such as Ria) depositing their cash proceeds over POL counters.

96. Romance and dating fraud: Action Fraud has seen a 26% increase in reports in 2020, with
the average victim of romance fraud losing just over £10,000. Losses reported by victims
between August 2019 and August 2020 totalled £66,335,239. Criminals are targeting
victims via dating sites and are then encouraging them to send money via money transfer
companies such as MoneyGram. City of London Police have been working with money
transfer companies to block suspicious transfers.

F. Conclusions and Recommendations

97. The regulatory environment continues to pose a challenge, with increased regulatory and
legislative focus on money laundering and terrorist financing. The 2020 NRA published in
December 2020 demonstrates risks remain high, particularly in relation to cash based
services, and calls out in the executive summary the risks associated with banking cash
deposits over POL counters. We expect HMT to introduce an Economic Crime Levy in 2021,
and this will increase the regulatory fee burden for POL and increase scrutiny from law
enforcement and supervisors as funding from the levy is applied to target money laundering
risks.

98. Covid has had a significant impact on the Financial Crime landscape, and there has been a
general increase in frauds, scams and changes in Organised Criminal Gang's (OCGs)
activities across industry. There has been an increase in scams relating to vulnerable
customers and particularly linked to Amazon vouchers and we are supporting work by the
product team to enhance controls. We have seen examples of suspicions activity in POL
related to potential misuse of BBL by prospective agents, and have worked with the Agent
On-boarding teams to implement controls. The volume of SARs have doubled and
investigations risen by a third in the last 12 months, leading to the recruitment in October
of an additional PO grade into the Financial Crime Compliance team funded until the end of
2020/21. There is no indication that volumes are reducing, and in September and October
SAR volumes were almost treble the average seen in 2019, therefore this resource will need
to be fully funded for 2021/22.

Tab 7 Money Laundering Annual Report

@

99. Significant focus has been given over the last 12 months by the NECC and Post Office in
addressing the risks relating to cash deposit services, but as more banks close and more
bank customers use Post Office the risks continue to grow, and Post Office needs to be able
to demonstrate that it is both aware of the risks, and has controls in place to manage them.
In 2020 up to P8 there have been 90 investigations involving c. £198m suspicious cash
deposits - the amount of suspicious deposits is more than double that for the whole of
2019. We have continued to support the NECC Project Admiralty established to tackle the
cash deposit threat over POL counters, and at the December meeting, 4 participating banks
confirmed that they have improved their account monitoring relating to these transactions.
We also established a BFS AML Sub Group in February to highlight and discuss the issues
with the banks, and improvements to the BFS agreement to more explicitly call out the
responsibilities of POL and the banks in relation to regulatory matters have been drafted
and submitted for review and comment by the end of January 2021.

100. Cash deposit values seen in 2020 relating to Money Transmission Businesses have
highlighted an unforeseen issue with businesses that deposit excessive volumes of cash
through a single location. Whilst actions have been taken to address the physical security
risks associated with these, further consideration is needed to tackle the money laundering
placement risks associated with accepting high value cash deposits from high risk
businesses. A robust solution needs to be identified and put in place to ensure that Post
Office can demonstrate to regulators, if called upon to do so, that sufficient controls are in
place.

101. The HMRC supervisor who had overseen Post Office regulated activity since 2015 retired in
May 2020 following handover to a new supervisor. A joint meeting was held in February to
demonstrate the controls that Post Office has in place in respect of Bureau de Change.
Unfortunately, the new supervisor secured a new role in the summer and a further
supervisor was appointed in August. An initial virtual meeting has been held, but due to
continuing Covid restrictions, HMRC are not undertaking any supervisory visits or meetings,
and it is unclear what level of scrutiny we may see in 2021. We are also expecting a
further consultation on HMRC’s fee structure in 2021.

102. Training is a key AML control for Post Office. There have been some instances of poor/late
completion of mandatory training by employees and contractors, but these issues are being
addressed with the assistance of Learning & Development. Completion will be monitored
more closely in 2021. We still see instances where SARs should have been raised by
branches, and more awareness raising via Area Managers is planned. The resource
responsible for training, awareness and communications was removed in November 2020
as a result of OE, and whilst the Communications Team will still support communication
activity, the work to support training content will have to be undertaken primarily by
Financial Crime Compliance moving forward, and this may impact the ability to further
enhance training.

103. Both first and second line management have responsibility to ensure that the controls in
place work as intended. Quarterly assurance checks against the policy minimum control
standards by Financial Crime Compliance evidence that a number of first line controls are
only partially effective. Processes within Compliance are robust and up to date, however
processes and policies across the business to support these are less mature and the planned
activity in 2021 by Compliance on the Controls Framework should help improve first line
understanding and application of their responsibilities.

104. Products and services provided by Post Office are broadly in line with the risk appetites set
by the Board and there has been a general improvement in residual risk over the last 12
months. The introduction of the new Financial Crime engagement tool simplifies the initial
