The Committee is asked to review the report, noting the Procurement Risk Exceptions submitted
to the Post Office Limited Group Executive and Board since November 2020 and to consider
and give direction in respect of the contracts in the Procurement pipeline which are high value
and at risk of being awarded or extended non-compliantly.

Previous Governance Oversight

e September 2020 - Board Report
e November 2020 - RCC & ARC Report

Executive Summary

As a business in receipt of public funds Post Office Limited (POL) is bound by the Public Contract
Regulations (2015). PCR 2015 oblige POL to behave in a fair, objective & transparent way when
contracting with 3" party suppliers. Additionally, set procedures must be followed for spend
above £25k and £189k.

The purpose of this report is to set out both breaches to Post Office governance and key controls
around contracts and compliance to PCR regulation in the award of contracts.

The aim of collating this information is to drive improvement in awareness and compliance
behaviour across the organisation. The second and primary aim is to work with GE and Business
Units to commence commercial reviews in a more timely way ensuring POL obtains value,
commercial and contractual flexibility fitting the requirements and business strategy of the
organisation.

In March 2020, Post Office Board requested prior approval of all Exceptions. This was revised
in September 2020 to above threshold Exceptions >£189k only in a revision to existing
governance. From November 2020 sub threshold exceptions will be submitted to the Group
Executive for prior approval and reported retrospectively to RCC and to ARC.

A Procurement Risk Exception Note is required to accompany all Exception Requests and a
Legal Risk note for requests >£189k.

Report

4. What are the potential consequences of non-compliant awards?

a) Pre-contractual remedies overview: During a Procurement, an aggrieved party can
seek an interim injunction suspending the tender or the implementation until the court
decides on an outcome.

b) Post-contractual remedies: The court can order an ‘ineffectiveness order’ rendering
the contract void &/or can award damages.

5. Why are these incidents of non-compliance occurring, and what can be done about it?
Non-compliant awards may be made for a number of reasons at the Post Office.

a) Low value, time constrained or highly sensitive/specialist engagements are not
uncommon.

b) Large commercial arrangements cannot often be easily competed or unravelled
without operational impact, and re-procurement may be subject to a pending evolution
of a supporting Business Strategy and/or completion of large, and complex technical
programmes of work to maintain or enhance services prior to a possible exit.

c) The contractual arrangements may pre-date PCR 2015 regulations or the contract
novated during separation from RMG, automatically becoming non-compliant at the
renewal point. Non-compliant awards are frequently made on a tactical basis to extend
contractual services while public tender processes are executed.

d) Delays to public sector panels of suppliers becoming available. The Post Office makes
extensive use of this low-cost route to market and new/refreshed panels are subject
to frequent delays from Crown Commercial Services. Single interim extensions [of
periods under 12 months] while tender processes are run are considered to be low
risk legally.

e) Changes in scope or value over the term of a contract may render the extension or
renewal of services non-compliant. Material changes to the scope of a contract may
render the whole contract non-compliant.

f) Disregard for, or lack of understanding of the regulations.

6. Why are we receiving this report?
A decision to collate this information into a single location was taken in the Autumn of
2016. The aim is to track and improve our overall compliance and commercial results as
an organisation, while also ensuring perceptions are accurate. However, it should be noted
that it will facilitate timely responses to Freedom of Information requests which adds risk
to the Post Office commercial landscape.

7. Are any of these breaches arguable on regulatory grounds or are they all breaches?

A full explanation of the individual compliance breaches for direct awards over £189k
[previously £164k & £181k] threshold is attached in Appendix 1. Each entry details the
nature of, and the value of the breach. The threshold is altered every two years based on
the FX rate between GBP and the Euro.

The Procurement Compliance Register does not at present give an indicative risk level
attached to the award. This information is provided to the accountable executives under
internal governance processes in the form of a PCR risk note before a contract above
threshold is entered into, and if necessary, under Legal Privilege. In addition, all
signatories to a contract have sight of the Risk note as part of the Contract Authorisation
Form [CAF].

All entries are compliance breaches. A period of challenge applies to each PCR breach once
an aggrieved party becomes aware or ought to have become aware. This risk finally
expires at 6 years from the date of breach. The defensibility of a legal challenge is outlined
within a Risk Note.

All contracts entered into during this period were compliant with internal governance
processes on contract and commercial review.

The rationale for approval is relevant to the individual service and is detailed within
Appendix 1.

10. What were the unapproved, material breaches?
There were no unapproved, material breaches during this period.

11. Describe what you are doing about the breaches. Where we are in breach, do we have a
plan to come back into compliance and over what time period will that plan take effect?
a) A forward view of material contracts falling under each Business Unit is currently

prepared by the relevant Procurement Manager for discussions with their key
stakeholders. The maturity of this look ahead view does vary currently and is
consistently a high priority activity within the team.

b) Sourcing options papers are prepared for review by contract managers and key
stakeholders [risk, legal, security] with routes to market agreed. In many cases these
are dependent on evolving business and operating model strategies and the
Procurement team are actively involved helping to advise and review options as
thinking evolves.

c) Where a non-compliant award is proposed due to time pressure, Procurement are
actively working on long term mitigation with awards made on an interim basis to
meet urgent operational needs.

d) Each RCC member now receives a regular report on compliance within their business
unit[s].

e) A Risk & Governance process requires a Risk Exception report to be created for non-
compliant direct awards with GE sign off.

f) Awards over £189k must have prior Board approval before being entered into.

g) All Professional Services engagements must be approved in writing in advance by the
CFO/COO. A compliant panel of preferred consulting partners has been appointed and
proposed engagements outside of this panel are subject to additional review and
challenge.

h) Procurement provides training as part of the revised Induction process for new staff.
Training packs are being updated for existing staff and a new training module made

Risk Assessment, Mitigations & Legal Implications

12. As a business in receipt of public funds POL is bound by the Public Contract Regulations
(2015). PCR 2015 oblige POL to behave in a fair, objective & transparent way when
contracting with 3" party suppliers. Additionally, set procedures must be followed for
spend above £25k and £189k.

13. Failure to abide by the legislation or “slicing and dicing” contracts exposes POL to risk,
both as far the commercial outcomes of the contracts as well as the reputational damage,
legal remedies, censure & fines that can follow the discovery of a breach. Our compliance
to PCR can be requested under a Freedom of Information request at any time.

14. The PCR Compliance Register allows for the tracking of breaches to PCR regulations at the
Post Office and internal governance processes. One aim of collating this information is to
drive improvement in awareness and compliance behaviour across the organisation. The
second and primary aim is to work with GE and Business Units to commence commercial
reviews in a more timely way ensuring POL obtains value, commercial and contractual
flexibility fitting the requirements and business strategy of the organisation.

15. Contract and financial governance policy and processes at Post Office are set by the Legal,
Risk and Governance team with clear guidelines for staff availably on the Company
Secretariat team intranet site. This sets out steps to be taken to obtain financial and

contractual approvals prior to making a binding commitment to an external party. Non-
compliance to internal governance processes are also captured within this report.

Tab 17 Law & Trends Update 17
POST OFFICE LIMITED
RISK & COMPLIANCE COMMITTEE REPORT
in. Meeting
Title: Law & Trends Report Date: 12 January 2021 I
Author: Sarah Gray, Group Legal Director Sponsor: Ben Foat, Group General Counsel

Input Sought: Noting

The Committee is asked to note the new or proposed material changes to laws and regulations
since the last Risk & Compliance Committee (RCC), for onward submission to the Audit, Risk &
Compliance Committee (ARC).

Executive Summary

There are 5 matters for the Committee to note (details of which are set out in the Appendix):

1. European Commission publishes new (draft) standard contractual clauses
New standard contractual clauses have been published to address the new requirements arising
from the ruling on the Schrems II case to ensure an adequate level of protection on data transfers.
ICO has not yet advised whether to adopt this approach in a Post Brexit Environment. The DPA
team will continue to monitor developments and report through the Law & Trends Forum as when
there are further updates.

2. Information Commissioners Office publishes new guidance on Data Subject
Access Requests
Following public consultation, new guidance has been published to help organisations respond to
the increasingly costly and time-consuming requirement to respond to subject access requests.
The new guidance is a positive for Post Office and hopefully makes the process simpler when
trying to manage the increased burden of responding to DSARs.

3. London Interbank Offered Rate (‘LIBOR’) Transition
Market is transitioning away from the interest rate benchmark LIBOR to a preferred alternative
in order to remove all dependencies by the end of 2021. Certain loans to which Post Office is party
to, as borrower and as lender, contain LIBOR rates and these will be reviewed and amended
before the end of 2021.

4. Reintroduction of the Crown Preference
HMRC has become a second ranking preferential creditor in respect of certain taxes and therefore
in the event of insolvency will recover debts owed to it ahead of some other creditors.

5. Revenue and Customs Brief No.12 - VAT Treatment on Compensation Payments
- Update
HMRC published its Revenue and Customers Brief 12 (2020) that introduced their new position
on VAT treatment of contractual compensation payments. The result of this approach by HMRC
will mean that most compensation, early termination and cancellation payments will be

i,

considered as payments for supply and will be liable for VAT. The Appendix updates on work done
thus far. Our review has shown that the previous GLO settlements are not impacted nor the MDA,

be issued.

6. Revenue and Customs Brief No.12 - VAT Treatment on Lease Breaks
Our review highlighted only one break penalty was paid between 1 October 2017 and 1 October
2020. Although VAT was charged, a VAT invoice will need to be issued accordingly.

1. European
Commission
publishes draft
new standard
contractual
clauses

(’sces')

‘As reported at RCC in September
2020, the Court of Justice of the
European Union (‘CJEU') issued a
final judgment that invalidated the
EU:US Privacy Shield Framework as
a transfer mechanism for transfers
of personal data to the US, however
ruled that SCCs were to remain a
valid mechanism of data transfer
outside of the EU.

The impact of the judgement was
softened by the promise that the
European Data Protection Board
would modernise the SCCs so that
they aligned with the General Data
Protection Regulations (‘GDPR’)
brought in during 2018.

On 12 November the European
Commission published the long awaited
new (draft) SCCs for the transfer of
personal data outside the EEA. Once
approved, companies a one-year grace
period to implement the new SCCs .

The new SCCs adopt a modular approach
which enables data exporters and
importers to select the clauses that are
relevant to the types of transfers they
engage in, with all major transfer
situations covered.

The main difference in the new SCCs is
that they place a duty on the data
exporter to document a transfer risk
assessment in all cases, with no
allowance made for low risk transfers.

At this time, it is hard to comment on the true
impact of the new SCCs given the UK's official
departure from the EU on the 31 December.

The impact is dependent on whether the
Information Commissioner's Office (‘ICO’),
the UK regulator, decides to adopt the new
SCCs so that we remain aligned with the EU
regulations in place or not.

If the ICO decide to adopt these clauses, Post
Office could be looking at another
remediation exercise across its contracts to
replace where the old SCCs were used, as
well as documenting the transfer impact
statements. This could be a very resource
heavy exercise to complete.

The ICO have published a statement that
they are considering its position and will take
a pragmatic and proportionate approach,
however they have not given any idea of
when they intend to make a final decision.

It is important to note that this is an area that
historically has not attracted fines from the
ICO, instead a ruling that the appropriate
documentation needs to be put in place.

The Data Protection team
continue to work with the legal
team to keep abreast of any
developments and ensure
these are built into any
upcoming contracts.

Further updates will be
provided to the Law and
Trends Forum as appropriate.

2. Information
Commissioners
Office publishes
new guidance
on Data Subject
Access

The right of access to one’s personal
data is a fundamental right under
data protection law and since the
introduction of the GDPR more
people have been exercising this
right.

The ICO launched a consultation in

The guidance does not seek to make
wholesale changes but ~— makes
clarifications or provides further detailed
guidance that is aimed to assist
companies when responding to DSARs.

There are three key developments in the
new guidance:

The new guidance is a positive for Post Office
and hopefully makes the process simpler
when trying to manage the increased burden
of responding to DSARs.

The key for Post Office is to ensure it applies
a consistent approach, whilst always
considering the context of each DSAR

The Data Protection team will
ensure the guidance is
implemented and — are
considering developing a
pricing matrix should Post
Office choose to request a
reasonable fee for responding
to excessive and unfounded

organisation must ensure the
reasonable fee is proportionate
whilst considering a number of
factors such as staff time and
photocopying costs. The guidance
encourages organisations —_to
consider a consistent formulaic
approach should it wish to charge a
reasonable fee.

3. London
Interbank
Offered

CLIBOR’)
Transition

Rate

UBOR is the London Interbank
Offered Rate. It is a benchmark
interest rate calculated to produce
an average of the rates at which
large banks can borrow money from
each other.

Currently, the interest rate in most
commercial loans between
companies and banks is calculated
by reference to LIBOR. It is also
widely used in many other financial
products and is available in 5
currencies, including sterling.

The use of LIBOR is coming to an end
because most banks are not funded by
borrowing from each other and therefore
LIBOR no longer reflects the market.
LIBOR will cease to be in effect from 31
December 2021 and an appropriate
replacement must be found.

The recommended replacement for
LIBOR in the UK is the Sterling Overnight
Indexed Average (‘SONIA’).

Certain loans to which Post Office is party to,
as borrower and as lender, contain LIBOR
rates and these will have to be reviewed and
amended before the end of 2021.

Post Office's working capital facility from
BEIS is currently being revised and the
transition from LIBOR is under negotiation.

‘Over the coming months, the
Legal and Finance team will
review the relevant
agreements and those that
refer to LIBOR will be
amended.

The expectation is that
commercial agreements will
not be affected, but this will be
checked and confirmed.

SONIA reflects the average interest rates
that banks pay to transact sterling
overnight from other _ financial
institutions. The key difference between
SONIA and LIBOR is that SONIA is
backwards looking and is calculated each
day, whereas LIBOR is forward looking
and is determined at the start of an
interest period.

basis in the event of insolvency of a

HMRC's position as an ordinary
unsecured creditor meant it came
below preferential creditors and
secured creditors with a floating
charge in the order of recovery.

The Crown Preference now makes HMRC
a secondary preferential creditor for
certain taxes (VAT, PAYE and Employees’
National Insurance), meaning recoveries
in insolvency will be allocated to these
HMRC debts ahead of many other
creditors. In turn this could reduce the
amounts that can be recovered by
floating charge holders and unsecured
creditors, which may mean they are less
willing to extend credit, especially for
certain businesses with high VAT or PAYE
liabilities.

Those in the restructure space see this
change as rather unhelpful in assisting
companies in danger, as floating charge
lenders are likely to lend less money
given the smaller bucket of assets
recoverable.

trading headwinds. It could impact Post
Office if those in its supply chain were to go
insolvent in that it could receive less than it
would otherwise.

The following industries are the most likely to
be impacted:

Recruitment;
Wholesale;
Importers/Exporters;
Construction; and
Manufacturing.

5. Revenue and
Customs Brief
No.12 - VAT
Treatment on

Compensation

On 2 September, HMRC published
its Revenue and Customers Brief 12

(2020) that introduced their new
position on VAT treatment of
contractual compensation
payments.

The updated position means that
VAT is applicable for:

Until recently, HMRC typically took the
view that as compensation or damages
payments made under a contract were
not consideration for a supply of goods
and services, they were therefore outside
the scope of VAT.

Following two recent EU judgements,
HMRC has now reversed this position, so
that many compensation payments or

matters to establish potential lial

insurers and external property

litigation lawyers to identify any potential
matters.

The insurers did not consider they had any
settlement payments which would be caught

Post Office Legal and Tax have
reviewed what we consider to
be the high-risk areas. In line
with most other corporates,
Post Office intends to await
the further guidance expected
from HMRC before undertaking
reviews into a wider group of
agreements.

The reasoning provided is that

early termination fees will fall within the

by the new guidance; insurance claims are
typically excluded.

Property litigation payments is the area likely
to be most affected by the guidance. HMRC
are due to publish more guidance on the
property aspect of their decision shortly and
once this has been published a decision will
be made whether to instruct the external
property lawyers to undertake a full review
of Post Office's historic property matters.

The new guidance was also considered in
light of payments made arising from the
Group Litigation (‘GLO Settlement’):

The GLO Settlement is silent to VAT,
hence the settlement sums payable
pursuant to it should be construed
as being inclusive of VAT.

Other settlements made during
2020 (‘Further Settlements’) are
either silent like the GLO Settlement
or provide expressly that the
Claimant Postmasters shall be
responsible for any tax on the
settlement sums.

On the above basis, no further payment is
required to be made by Post Office in respect
of VAT on the GLO Settlement or Further
Settlements.

The position on further settlements under the

Historical

Shortfall Scheme is slightly

different. The matter is being considered by
KPMG, and the preliminary view is that no
VAT should be payable however they are
giving the matter further consideration.

The outcome of the above review found that
in relation to the MDA and HPBB, VAT was
recoverable and therefore if a contractual
compensation payment were to be made, the
commercial terms for both contracts cover
the new VAT treatment. In relation to the BFA
all activity was VAT exempt and therefore
this change in VAT treatment is not
applicable. This means there is no immediate
concern for any of the above agreements.
